The map also features directional arrows, a legend explaining site features and an insert table providing explanation to a numbered features on the island]Figure Figure 5‑1 Significance of Site Elements

## 6. Management Analysis

The information and analysis presented in the preceding chapters of this HMP shows that Finance has a responsibility for the heritage values of Snapper Island, as assessed against the CHL criteria.

This section presents and discusses the range of potential risks from the interaction of day-to-day and strategic Finance activities and the heritage values of the place.

### 6.1 Remediation Requirements

A structural assessment was undertaken in July 2023 (Macquarie Consulting Engineers 2023) and determined that only four of the remaining buildings, along with the jetty and ramp, are in a reasonably sound condition:

• Building 2 – Guard House;

• Building 3 – Officers’ Wardroom Mess;

• Building 10 – Main Deck; and

• Building 14 – Officer’s Cottage.

Most of the other elements of these buildings are structurally sound but require some maintenance to repair damaged elements and fabric which, if left unattended, will accelerate their deterioration.

The 2023 structural assessment found that repairs to Building 4 and 5 (Storage Room and Women’s Toilet), Building 6 (Boatshed, Workshop and Store), Building 8 (Gymnasium), Building 9 (J. Payne Memorial Building), and the BBQ area would be difficult and costly. The Structural Integrity Report states that structural repairs to Building 4,5, 6, 8 and 9 are not considered practically achievable and the buildings are therefore non-recoverable. Buildings 6 and 8 are currently inaccessible due to safety considerations:

• Building 4 and 5 (Storage Room and Women’s Toilet Room) are described as dilapidated and not recoverable and a decision regarding demolition or restoration will be required (subject to approval);

• Building 6 (Boatshed, Workshop and Store) is reaching a state of deterioration which necessitates a decision to be made in the short term whether to demolish this building or develop a remediation and restoration strategy to mitigate its continued decay (subject to approval); and

• Building 8 (Gymnasium) and Building 9 (J. Payne Memorial Building) have severe damage to their foundations and underfloor, particularly evident in the collapse of the sick bay in Building 9 (J. Payne Memorial Building) and require immediate remediation to preserve or a decision regarding possible restoration and reconstruction, or demolition (subject to approval).

Many of the timber walkways around and between buildings are also in a state of severe disrepair. Temporary remediation activities such as the installation of marine ply and grip tape overlays have been recently undertaken to provide safe access in key areas, however a more permanent solution will have to be implemented if continued access around the island is required.

The concrete apron around Building 10 (Main Deck) and other concrete paths, retaining walls, and stairs around the island are also heavily damaged, generally due to the root growth of invading vegetation. Removal of this vegetation and repair of the concrete should also be a remediation priority.

The sea walls around the perimeter of the island are also dilapidated and unstable with partial failure in localised sections resulting in erosion of the shoreline. Significant works are required to stabilise the shoreline and reverse the loss to erosion.

A Remediation Action Plan (RAP) was developed for the site in May 2024 and includes an assessment of remedial options that reduces the potential health and environmental risks of the contamination on site to an acceptable level. The current RAP also includes cost estimates for options to inform decision making on prudent use of public funds.

Further information about condition and immediate maintenance and repairs at Snapper Island is provided in Sections 6.10 and 7.12.

### 6.2 Safety

There are currently safety issues concerning access to and around some of the buildings on the Island. Access issues have been created by walkways and stairs being in disrepair, overgrown vegetation, and accumulation of items and material around the island creating hazards.

A general ‘clean-up’ of the Island was recommended to remove disused and deteriorating timbers, scrap metals and other types of rubbish. Initial works were undertaken by ERM and Australia’s Insurance Builders (AIB), under instruction from Finance, in March 2019. Additional ‘clean-up’ was undertaken in 2023. These works were able to control some of the overgrown vegetation, remove a significant volume of rubbish and scrap materials from the island, significantly improving access in many areas. Access restrictions still apply in some areas where walkways are in disrepair or buildings are too deteriorated to access. Appropriate signage is displayed on buildings that cannot be entered.

Silver gull and pigeon infestation has also created health and safety issues and access constraints to some buildings and parts of the island. Seagull management actions have been undertaken since 2020, in line with the recommendations of the Ecosure (2020) Seagull Management Plan.

The PSP manages safety risks through regular inspections for WHS risk assessments of the site and preparation of a WHS management plan. A safety induction package has been prepared by the PSP and all personnel including contractors are required to complete the induction before accessing the site.

### 6.3 Public Access

Finance does not allow public access to Snapper Island or its buildings due to the current condition of walkways, seawalls and buildings and the associated safety concerns. Unauthorised entry has been deterred using Danger and Warning signs notifying of asbestos and other safety concerns.

If public access to Snapper Island is to be allowed in the future, the condition of the buildings would need to be greatly improved. Minimum safety requirements should be met, including reference to the recommendations provided in Table 7‑1. If public access is to be promoted in future use decisions, conservation and restoration of all buildings and features (as guided by this HMP) should be undertaken to make them practical and desirable for future use.

No disability access is afforded to the upper deck of Snapper Island or Building 9 (J. Payne Memorial Building). Disability access could be considered through the installation of a lift or ramp; however, its location would need to consider impacts to the significance of the fabric of the building/ element and overall significance of the Island.

Visitor numbers at Snapper Island may result in damage to heritage items and sites, including vandalism and souveniring. To mitigate this risk, the heritage interpretation of Snapper Island should act to educate visitors of the values and significance of the Island.

Section 6.2 provides further guidance on the management of safety and access to Snapper Island.

### 6.4 Change in Ownership

Finance is currently in discussion with SHFT regarding the future management of Snapper Island. Properties that are considered surplus to Finance requirements are disposed of in accordance with the requirements of the Commonwealth Property Disposals Policy, which is administered by Finance.

If Finance executes a contract for the sale or lease of Snapper Island then the Environment Minister would be notified by Finance in accordance with Section 341ZE of the EPBC Act. Additionally, Finance would work with the new owner to agree on conditions of transfer that would allow for ongoing protection of identified heritage values post sale.

If the sale/lease/transfer of the Island was not to another Commonwealth agency (such as SHFT) then, as the place has been assessed as meeting more than one criterion of the SHR, Finance would include a nomination of the site to the NSW Heritage Council. In this case, consultation with Heritage NSW should also be undertaken early to support a streamlined process for ongoing heritage protection.

Section 7.4 provides further guidance relating to changing ownership of Snapper Island.

### 6.5 Future Use Opportunities and Constraints

Snapper Island is currently unoccupied, and does not have an ongoing use identified. Discussions are underway between Finance and SHFT about the future management of the Island. The SHFT’s Comprehensive Plan (see Section 3.1.5), approved by the Environment Minister, identifies possible future uses for the Island. Any future use of Snapper Island would consider allowing public access, subject to safety and security restrictions.

Once the future use and management of the Site has been agreed, it is important that the personnel responsible for Site management and maintenance are familiar with the requirements of a heritage site. Information contained in this HMP can be utilised for training materials that can be included in safety inductions for groups undertaking any activities on Snapper Island and briefings to maintenance contractors.

The Snapper Island – Exhibition of Discussion Paper and Draft Conservation Management Plan Consultation Outcomes Report (SHFT, 2021) detailed various future uses for Snapper Island as suggested by the public. SHFT noted public support for the outcomes provided in the Comprehensive Plan (detailed in Section 3.1.5). Some future uses suggested in the report included:

• Re-establishment of maritime training, involving use by Navy/cadet community;

• Sailing/base for sailing events;

• Aboriginal and historic (Navy/military) interpretation;

• Boat building/maritime skills for young people;

• Men’s Shed/Women’s Shed;

• Arts and crafts;

• Food and beverage (kiosk, café, function centre);

• Accommodation;

• Facilities for day-tripping (picnics/playground);

• Showcase for sustainable building practices;

• Water-based recreational activities (kayaking, sailing, fishing); and

• Guided tours/activities that collaborate with those associated with Cockatoo and Spectacle Islands.

SHFT stated that maritime activities would be encouraged at Snapper Island (in accordance with the Comprehensive Plan) should the site be transferred to their ownership.

The Heritage Interpretation Strategy at Appendix E provides recommendations to support interpretation of the Site as part of its conservation. These include the recommendation to prepare a more detailed Heritage Interpretation Plan to implement on site interpretation in the context of suitable future uses and safe access.

The sections below provide a discussion of some suggested short-term, mid-term and long-term scenarios for future uses of Snapper Island. Sections 7.5 and 7.5.1.1 provide further guidance relating to future use opportunities and development constraints.

#### 6.5.1 Short-Term Priorities

To maximise reuse opportunities remediation and critical repairs (of remaining faults) should be addressed for public safety (refer to Sections 6.9 and 7.12) in the short-term.

Once adaptive reuse opportunities of the Island or buildings are decided, a detailed building conservation and restoration plan should be developed and implemented, in partnership with maritime and naval heritage organisations.

The options and opportunities outlined in the Heritage Interpretation Strategy (Appendix E) at Snapper Island could inform future use planning for the Site. In the short-term, content and images provided in the Heritage Interpretation Strategy can be included on Finance or the new owner’s website to assist with communicating the heritage values of Snapper Island.

#### 6.5.2 Mid-Term Scenarios

Future use options for Snapper Island should retain its heritage values. Conservation opportunities exist for some of buildings on the island; those that have high Commonwealth Heritage values including Building 10 (Main Deck) and Building 2 (Guard House) should be conserved. Original fabric and internal layouts of these buildings should be conserved, and new uses found for these buildings.

As outlined in Section 6.1, there would be considerable intervention work required to enable stabilisation and retention of several assets on the Island. The extent of work likely to be required is such that reconstruction rather than restoration would be required, which would require careful cost benefit analysis including a HIA as part of the EPBC Act self-assessment process to determine if the proposed works would require a referral.

There is a possibility of reconstruction and adaptation for buildings of moderate and low significance, such as reconstruction of Building 6 (Boatshed, Workshop and Store) or adaptation of Building 8 (Gymnasium) for an alternative purpose, subject to a HIA and analysis of the prudence and feasibility of the extent of work required. These could include maritime and community uses as listed at Section 6.5 above. Modification and alteration, including replacement of materials and features, would be permissible. For elements of low significance, additional modification and alteration, including replacement of materials and features, would be permissible to allow new uses. Removal of these elements and those not prudent and feasibly repairable could also be considered. Further discussion of adaptation considerations is included in Section 6.10.

A potential new use for Building 10 (Main Deck) is a historical education facility, highlighting the former use as a military training/ educational facility. This future usage would resemble educational tourism, rather than reuse for occupation or intensive training activities. Once a future use has been determined, the Len Forsythe Museum collection could potentially be returned to the island and conserved. Following conservation and repairs, the collection could be housed in Building 10 (Main Deck).

Consideration could also be given to the use of or access to the island by a community organisation that could assist with the conservation and general care of the island, be involved in maintenance programs and educational/ heritage and military history awareness programs.

#### 6.5.3 Long-Term Scenarios

Following establishment of a sustainable use of the facilities at Snapper Island, consideration should be given to guided heritage tours (that may operate in association with those at Cockatoo and Spectacle Islands). The design and installation of signs and associated online content to be accessed digitally while on site would be a key aspect of introducing tours.

#### 6.5.4 Constraints

The retention of an appropriate visual setting and physical environment is an essential element of heritage conservation. The island retains the physical siting and general layout from its establishment and contributes to the landscape values of Sydney Harbour. The Site has a visual relationship with the surrounding islands in the harbour (Spectacle and Cockatoo Islands) along with the coastal locations of Drummoyne and Balmain (refer to Figure Figure 5‑1). These views to and from the island should be retained.

The layout of the island is integral to its significance and should be retained to maintain the integrity of the island through its intra-site patterning. As such, new development should be limited to and be sympathetic in scale to existing footprints where buildings have been removed or demolished, such as the former Building 12 (Signal Station), Building 11 (Mess Deck), Building 7 (Ablutions Block) and Building 1 (Flammable Store). Specific areas where new development should be avoided include the Forecastle Deck and After End.

The nature of any proposed new development should be assessed in terms of bulk, massing, form, scale, and materials, with a view to minimising impacts on the heritage values. Any new development, including additions to existing structures must be deferential to the building and contemporary in design.

### 6.6 Legislative Compliance

Compliance with the EPBC Act is required whilst in Commonwealth ownership. NSW legislation may apply to certain activities by the Commonwealth, and legal advice should be sought on whether approvals or permits would be required. NSW legislation would apply if the property were divested from Commonwealth ownership. Section 3 of this HMP outlines the planning framework relating to Snapper Island to ensure legislative compliance on heritage issues under the EPBC Act.

### 6.7 Climate Change

Climate change has the potential to have profound effects on the structure and function of many Australian ecosystems and buildings. These changing climatic conditions will result in increased temperatures, increased fire risk and increased intensity of storm events.

Climate change has the potential to cause damage to the heritage values of Snapper Island. Periodic monitoring should be undertaken, and a review of existing management strategies may be required in the event of changing climatic conditions to meet the changing pressures on the built environment. Adaptation measures could include increased weatherproofing to buildings such as carefully designed additional cladding, seawall reinforcement and storm surge infrastructure. These modifications would need to be designed with input from a heritage specialist and a Heritage Impact Assessment is likely to be required to support the approvals process.

### 6.8 Moveable Heritage

Artefacts belonging to the Len Forsythe Collection from the Museum at Snapper Island (Building 10 Main Deck) were removed in c.2006 and relocated to join other collections housed at Spectacle and Garden Islands. Subject to suitable conservation environment and agreement with the Department of Defence, some of these items could be loaned for future use in interpretation at Snapper Island.

In 2019, remaining items located in Buildings 9 (J. Payne Memorial Building), 10 (Main Deck) and 14 (Officer’s Cottage) were systematically sorted to determine which items could be removed and if any of the objects were of value and needed to be retained. In undertaking this cleaning process, priority was given to retaining items which could be utilised in interpretation of the island, such as signage, uniforms and objects associated with training. In addition, building materials or furniture that could be salvaged and utilised in refurbishing the island in the future were retained and stock-piled. Much of the remaining furniture, materials or objects considered to be in poor condition, contaminated by bird/vermin droppings or otherwise unusable were disposed of. This included mattresses, broken cupboards and display cases and anything subject to severe water damage.

A residual collection of items is predominantly currently located in Buildings 9 (J. Payne Memorial Building) and 10 (Main Deck); an inventory of those items with heritage significance (moveable heritage items) are detailed in Appendix H. This includes management recommendations for each item.

### 6.10 Changes to Significant Heritage Fabric

A qualified heritage specialist should be consulted for any proposed changes or major maintenance where impacts to significant fabric or values could occur. Refer to Sections 7.2, 7.3, 7.5, 7.11 and 7.12 for further guidance.

#### 6.10.1 Tolerance for Change

Managing places of heritage significance involves understanding which attributes of an element or a site contribute to its significance, and then assessing each of the attributes’ ‘tolerance for change’. For buildings and structures (or groups of buildings and structures), their form, fabric, function and/or location are usually the key attributes that embody their significance. To assist future planning and management of the site, specific attributes of the built and landscape components have been analysed and their ‘tolerance for change’ assessed.

While ‘tolerance for change’ levels can be a useful guide to conservation and development works and consider official heritage values, the Commonwealth (or National) Heritage values as identified in the CHL are to take precedence in planning change.

The analysis of contributory heritage elements described in this report assists with managing any proposed change to Snapper Island’s heritage values. Contributory elements have been assessed against their grades of significance for sensitivity or ‘tolerance for change’ to identify the extent to which their heritage values are able to tolerate alteration without adversely affecting the nature or degree of their significance to the whole-of-site values.

The rankings for tolerance for change are presented in below in Table 6‑2 and are derived from Burke (2010).

Table 6‑2 Tolerance for Change Definitions

| **Degree** | **Policy/Management Action** |
|---|---|
| **No tolerance for change – highly sensitive** | The key attributes (form, fabric, function, location, intangible values) strongly contribute to the heritage significance of the element and/or its contribution to the significance of the site. The element retains a high degree of integrity and authenticity with only very minor alterations that respect or enhance its significance.<br>The key attributes of the element or the site should be retained and conserved. Any change may result in adverse impact on the assessed significance of the element or site. |
| **Low tolerance for change** | The key attributes (form, fabric, function, location, intangible values) contribute to the heritage significance of the element and/or its contribution to the site. The element retains a moderate degree of integrity and authenticity with only minor alterations that respect its significance. |
| **Moderate tolerance for change** | The key attributes (form, fabric, function, location, intangible values) partly contribute to the heritage significance of the element and/or its contribution to the significance of the site; it may have undergone some alteration/change which does not detract from its authenticity and significance.<br>The key attributes of the element should be generally retained and conserved. Moderate change to specific attributes is possible provided there are only minimal adverse impacts and the assessed significance of the element, or the site overall is retained. |
| **High tolerance for change – nil/low sensitivity** | The key attributes (form, fabric, function, location, intangible values) of the element have relatively little individual heritage significance but may contribute to the overall significance of the site.<br>A greater level of change is possible to specific attributes of this element, avoiding adverse impacts and retaining the significance of the site overall. |

Table 6‑3 provides opportunities for conservation and development, highlighting the condition, heritage significance and tolerance for change ranking of contributory items at Snapper Island. The table provides detailed management guidance for each building, based on the assessed significance and tolerance for change of each element. Tolerance for change rankings for elements are shown in Figure 6‑1.

#### 6.10.2 Managing Significant Fabric

Site elements identified as significant may be highly intact, rare, or play a major part in contributing to the historical significance of a building or Snapper Island as a whole. Original and early fabric, both internally and externally, is of significance and should be retained and conserved.

Different parts of each building may have different levels of significance based on their age, condition, and integrity. Most structures on Snapper Island are constructed of timber (timber framed buildings with timber framed windows and doors) and corrugated galvanised iron (CGI) – walls and roofs. Other important fabric includes stone (sea walls and stairs to the upper level) and steel/ metals (eaves and the pre-fabricated framing of Building 8 [Gymnasium]). Some general guidance for the conservation and maintenance of this fabric is provided below. Detailed management recommendations for each building are provided in Table 6‑3 and Appendix A.

#### 6.10.3 Significant Fabric – General Approach

**Timber**

All buildings on Snapper Island contain some timber elements, for example, a building frame, window and door architraves, or a roof truss. Regular and consistent maintenance is pivotal to the care of historic timbers to prevent moisture penetration. This is best prevented through regular painting with an appropriate finish. Repairs should be undertaken by a carpenter and where timbers require replacement, always use the conservation approach of “like for like” and consider splicing to retain as much original fabric as possible. The NSW Heritage Office has a series of maintenance publications that includes timber repairs and wood preservation:

• Heritage maintenance: Timber repairs - Information sheet 5.2: https://www.environment.nsw.gov.au/research-and-publications/publications-search/heritage-maintenance-timber-repairs; and

• Heritage maintenance: Wood preservation Information sheet 5.1: https://www.environment.nsw.gov.au/research-and-publications/publications-search/heritage-maintenance-wood-preservation

Best practice publications for the conservation of historic timbers can also be found on the ICOMOS website:

• https://openarchive.icomos.org/id/eprint/1656/1/Conservation_of_Historic_Timber_Structures-2.pdf.

**Corrugated Iron and Steel/Metal**

Many of the buildings on Snapper Island have CGI wall sheets and/ or roofs, and/or metal eaves, gutters and downpipes or framing. Where repair or replacement is required, repair materials should match the existing. When CGI lap sheets have rusted around their fixings, it is recommended a slip sheet is inserted to extend the life of the roof or wall. Patching small holes by lead soldering a patch of iron over the hole will also extend the life of a sheet. The NSW Heritage Office has a series of maintenance publications that include the care of corrugated iron and steel and metal work:

• Heritage maintenance: Corrugated roofing Information sheet 4.1: https://www.environment.nsw.gov.au/research-and-publications/publications-search/heritage-maintenance-corrugated-roofing; and

• Heritage maintenance: Metalwork - Information sheet 3.1: https://www.environment.nsw.gov.au/research-and-publications/publications-search/heritage-maintenance-metalwork

[image: Aerial map showing Snapper Island in Sydney Harbour with color-coded zones indicating tolerance for change rankings of significant heritage elements, ranging from low (yellow) to high (orange) and demolished (grey). The map includes labelled site features with explanation to numbering on the buildings on the island, a legend, scale bar, and directional arrows, highlighting key structures like Forecastle Deck and Wharf End for heritage management planning.]

Figure 6‑1 Tolerance for Change Rankings of Significant Elements

## 7. Policies and Guidelines

This section presents policies and guidelines to address the on-going management and care of the heritage values at Snapper Island.

### 7.1 Management Objectives

The objective of future site management is to conserve the Commonwealth heritage values of Snapper Island in the context of a range of management requirements and issues. As the site is on the divestments list, Finance is exploring options to provide for the ongoing conservation of heritage values throughout the divestment process in accordance with EPBC Act requirements.

### 7.2 Approach to Heritage Management

The Burra Charter is widely recognised for its guiding principles on conservation in Australia in terms of the physical fabric. The Burra Charter contains basic conservation principles in a range of articles, which address cultural significance and how to protect it. This HMP advocates an understanding of the Burra Charter principles which include:

• the place itself is important;

• understand the significance of the place;

• understand the fabric;

• significance should guide decisions;

• do as much as is necessary, but as little as possible;

• keep records; and

• do everything in a logical order.

In the first instance, the HMP should be adopted by the owner. It is important that the personnel responsible for the management and maintenance of the Site are familiar with the requirements of a heritage site. Awareness of the matters to consider and procedures to follow is essential to the successful ongoing conservation management of the heritage place. A succinct heritage training and awareness package should be developed and provided to appropriate personnel and any contractors on an ongoing basis.

**Policy 1**

This HMP is to be adopted by the authorities responsible for the management of the Site and used in its ongoing strategic and day-to-day management.

**Policy 2**

The conservation and management of the heritage values of the Site should protect its CHL values and be carried out in accordance with the principles of the Burra Charter.

**Policy 3**

Seek advice from a suitably qualified and experience heritage specialist if proposed changes or maintenance have the potential to adversely impact the listed and assessed heritage values of the Site.

### 7.3 Management Framework and Works Approval

#### 7.3.1 Management of Contributory Elements

##### 7.3.1.1 Site Management Framework & Training

Snapper Island is currently part of the Finance Owned Estate. Overall responsibility of the Site’s management and maintenance resides with the PSP on behalf of Finance. All personnel responsible for the management and maintenance of the Site should be made aware of the heritage values it contains.

**Policy 4**

Heritage conservation awareness training should be developed and implemented for personnel responsible for the management and maintenance of the Site, including provision of ongoing access to the latest version of the HMP

##### 7.3.1.2 Detailed Management Guidelines for Contributory Elements

Detailed discussion of significant heritage fabric and their management is contained in Section 6.10. The following policies are a summary of the information contained in that Section.

**Policy 5**

Elements and fabric of high significance at Snapper Island should be retained, conserved, and protected where practicable and subject to consideration of building condition, with minimal intervention.

**Policy 6**

Any works that may impact on the significant fabric of Snapper Island should only be undertaken once an HIA has been prepared and any necessary EPBC Act approvals have been granted. All works must be undertaken by suitably qualified tradespeople familiar with heritage conservation.

**Policy 7**

The Maintenance outlined in Table 7‑1, as well as less extensive change, is permissible, provided it is undertaken in accordance with Section 7.12 and the HAMS by a tradesperson familiar with heritage conservation, this HMP, and is documented in accordance with Policies 33 and 34.

### 7.4 Change in Ownership

**Policy 8**

If part or all of Snapper Island is to be disposed of in the future, the relevant Commonwealth agency will notify the Environment Minister in accordance with Section 341ZE of the EPBC Act that a place listed on the CHL is to be sold.

**Policy 9**

Should be property be divested out of Commonwealth ownership, the relevant Commonwealth agency will ensure the ongoing protection of identified Commonwealth heritage values post-sale. As Snapper Island has been found to meet more than one criterion of the NSW State Heritage Register, this will include nomination of the site to the NSW Heritage Council. The HMP will also be provided to the purchaser.

### 7.5 Future Use and Adaptive Reuse Guidelines

Ideally, future use options for Snapper Island should consider the retention of its heritage values through usage which highlights the former use as a military training/ educational facility. This future usage could resemble educational tourism, rather than reuse for occupation or intensive training activities. Once a future use has been determined, the Len Forsythe Museum collection could potentially be returned to the island and conserved. Following conservation and repairs, the collection could be housed in Building 10 (Main Deck) and opened to the public.

**Policy 10**

A specific resolution concerning the future or adaptive reuse of the Site should be sought through a consultative process between Finance, potential new owners, and relevant stakeholders.

#### 7.5.1 Future Use and Adaptive Reuse

It is understood that there are considerable contaminants on the island (through the nature of the development of Snapper Island and via deteriorating building fabric) for which remediation recommendations have previously been posed. In addition, the impact of the marine environment and general lack of conservation, critical repairs, and general maintenance on the Island over the last decade has resulted in the significant deterioration of some of the buildings on the island. As such, prior to any considered future use, the island should be subject to remediation. In addition, prior to any considered future or adaptive reuse of the Island or buildings, at a minimum critical repairs (of remaining faults) should be addressed for the safety of those needing to access the island (refer to Section 7.12), and conservation and restoration of relevant buildings and features should be undertaken in accordance with this HMP and relevant approvals to make them practical and desirable for future use.

**Policy 11**

Critical repairs (of remaining faults) are immediately required for safety reasons (refer to Repairs Schedule at Table 7.1).

**Policy 12**

Conservation and restoration of all buildings and features are required prior to re-opening the Site to the public.

##### 7.5.1.1 Demolitions

The first option for heritage buildings that become redundant is for consideration of adaptive re-use. If no re-use can be determined, then the next option would be mothballing until a future time and re-use may become viable. The last resort option if no future use is viable is to seek their removal or demolition. Demolition to remove an ongoing maintenance requirement or because no funding will be allocated to provide basic ongoing maintenance for the asset is not sufficient justification. Additionally, demolition on the basis of Work Health and Safety is not sufficient justification alone, without examining alternatives.

The proponent must examine possible mitigation measures and demonstrate that there is no prudent and feasible alternative to demolition. This information must be included in a HIA, along with supporting documentation such as structural engineering assessments, quantity surveyor cost estimates for alternative options, and contamination reports (if relevant), as these will form the basis of the justification for the action in an EPBC Act referral. If demolition of multiple buildings is under consideration, then these should be presented as a package of works in the one EPBC referral. The HIA must also consider the cumulative impacts to the heritage values of the site from previous demolitions as well as the proposed demolitions.

**Policy 13**

All prudent and feasible alternatives to demolition must be examined when demolition of a heritage asset is proposed. Demolition to remove an ongoing maintenance requirement or Workplace Health and Safety (WHS) requirement is not sufficient justification alone.

**Policy 14**

Demolitions will be referred for a decision by the Environment Minister under the EPBC Act. The EPBC referral will be supported by documentation including HIA that explores alternatives and justifications, along with structural and contamination reports as relevant.

### 7.6 Development Controls

Future development opportunities at Snapper Island will arise out of a consultative process between the relevant Commonwealth agency and any other relevant stakeholders. All future development proposals should seek to retain and enhance existing heritage values as part of the Site planning process.

**Policy 15**

To retain the intra-site patterning on Snapper Island, new development should be limited to the footprints of removed or demolished buildings (Building 12 [Signal Station], Building 11 [Mess Deck], Building 7 [Ablution Block], and Building 1 [Flammable Store]).

**Policy 16**

New development should be avoided in the open areas of the Forecastle Deck and After End.

**Policy 17**

Future development proposals must consider the retention of significant view lines to and from the Island.

Design in a historic context aims to preserve the special qualities that give a place character in a way that respects the old while reflecting the new, and meeting the amenity needs of its users. A wide range of design solutions may emerge for any design problem after careful analysis of surrounding buildings and sympathetic interpretation of their design elements.

**Policy 18**

Any new development or additions to existing structures should be deferential to the existing buildings and contemporary in design. New buildings/ structures should consider the footprint, bulk, scale, and materials of the adjacent buildings on the island.

### 7.7 Climate Change Adaptation

Climate change is anticipated to result in increased temperatures, increased fire risk and increased intensity of storm events. Climate change has the potential to cause damage to the heritage values of Snapper Island.

**Policy 19**

Undertake periodic monitoring of Snapper Island, after climactic events such as king tides or storm surges, particularly the sea walls and those buildings close to the outer edges of the Island (Buildings 6, 8 & 9).

**Policy 20**

Ensure all site management personnel are familiar with the emergency response plan for Snapper Island and that communication and response protocols are clear to address damage from storms, king tides and other extreme weather events. Review the emergency response plan for Snapper Island annually, and update as needed.

**Policy 21**

In the event of damage caused by extreme weather events, undertake repairs with advice from suitably qualified experts including built heritage specialists and engineers as required as quickly as possible to prevent further damage.

**Policy 22**

Between 2023-2025 explore options for climate change adaptation works that can prevent damage that could be caused by extreme weather events while also avoiding and minimising potential adverse heritage impacts. Seek approval under the EPBC Act if adaptation works may have an adverse or significant heritage impact.

### 7.8 Chance Finds Management

It is relatively unlikely that the sub-surface areas of site contain items of archaeological interest that may be discovered during works on the Island given the extent of sub-surface disturbance involved in the construction of the Site. However, the buildings themselves provide some potential for unforeseen discovery of archaeological items.

The cultural heritage analysis of the Site (see Appendix G and Appendix I) has determined that it has low Aboriginal archaeological potential. However, if potential Aboriginal or historic archaeological items are found during site works, the protocols at Appendix J are to be followed.

In the event of the discovery of possible human remains, the Police must be contacted immediately and all works must cease.

**Policy 23**

In the event of an unforeseen discovery, the find should be reported immediately to the Site Manager and owner, and any works ceased immediately. Depending on the nature of the find, other authorities may need to be contacted. Refer to Appendix J for Chance Finds Protocol.

### 7.9 Interpretation and Access

#### 7.9.1 Interpretation

There is currently no interpretation of the heritage values of Snapper Island.

The purpose of interpretation of heritage places is to reveal and explain their significance and to enable that significance to be understood by the people that manage the place and the public that access it.

Potential interpretation opportunities exist in providing more easily accessible information about the island (e.g. an online presence/website) and perhaps allocating set “open days” or visiting hours where guided tours or information talks could be provided. Discrete interpretive panels or posters with historical photographs for reference could also be placed around the Island/ buildings to enhance interpretation not just for external visitors but also for the future users of the island.

A Heritage Interpretation Strategy has been developed for this HMP (provided at Appendix E).

**Policy 24**

Consider opportunities to improve the heritage interpretation at Snapper Island through the implementation of the interpretation strategy provided at Appendix E. Interpretation methods can include online information, open days and discrete interpretative panels around the island.

#### 7.9.2 Access and Safety

Concrete paths and wooden walkways across Snapper Island are in poor condition, often not at all accessible. The condition of the stairs, paths, and walkways should be improved in accordance with recommendations provided in Table 7‑1 to prevent potential safety incidents.

Access to the upper level of Building 9 (J. Payne Memorial Building) is currently restricted to a ladder owing to the removal of the internal stairs and the external ramp, removed by Finance after vacation of Snapper Island by the Sydney Training Depot Snapper Island Ltd.

Buildings 6 and 8 are also currently inaccessible due to safety considerations regarding the extent of damage to piers supporting these two structures.

**Policy 25**

Subject to the investigation of the prudence and feasibility of retention via a HIA, safety improvements to buildings at Snapper Island focused on essential stablisation should be undertaken immediately. Repair of concrete paths and timber walkways should be a maintenance priority, if deemed safe and practical to do so.

**Policy 26**

Subject to the investigation of the prudence and feasibility of retention via a HIA, access to the upper level of Building 9 (J. Payne Memorial Building) should be reinstated if it is safe to do so via either (or both) internal stairs or an external walkway.

Access to the upper level of the island is currently via a set of stone stairs. No disability access is afforded to these areas. Disability access could be considered through the installation of a lift or ramp; however, location would need to consider impacts to the significance of the fabric of the building/ element and overall significance of the Island. Any works or the adaptive reuse of the Island must consider current equitable access obligations under the *Disability Discrimination Act 1992.*

**Policy 27**

Any works and the adaptive reuse of Snapper Island should consider equitable access obligations under the Disability Discrimination Act 1992.

**Policy 28**

Conservation and restoration of all buildings and features to enable public access to Snapper Island (as guided by this HMP) should be carefully designed to facilitate safe access and avoid adverse impacts to heritage values.

### 7.10 Stakeholder Consultation

With the need to ensure compliance under the EPBC Act for heritage matters, consultation with a range of stakeholders will be required from time to time. Where an activity is proposed that may impact heritage values (and may require an EPBC referral), early consultation with DCCEEW is advised to ensure all timelines for approvals are factored into work programs.

Consultation with Aboriginal stakeholders will also be required to inform them about activities that affect aspects of Aboriginal cultural heritage on Snapper Island. Given no known heritage values are present on the island this will most likely relate to matters for the interpretation of Aboriginal cultural heritage. This should also include a contact protocol in the event of a chance find being discovered during works at the site.

**Policy 29**

Consult with key stakeholders on matters that may involve heritage impacts, keep them informed of decisions regarding changes to heritage values at Snapper Island, and involve them in future updates to the HMP.

**Policy 30**

Contact the Traditional Owners (at present, the Metropolitan LALC) if there are any unexpected Aboriginal heritage finds or any incidents that could impact Aboriginal heritage values. If intangible cultural heritage values are identified through consultation with Traditional Owners in the future, facilitation of safe access to the site regarding these values should be provided. Management of any culturally sensitive information that may be identified in the future is to be agreed with the Traditional Owners and implemented accordingly.

### 7.11 Monitoring, Recording and Reporting Protocols

The objectives of this section of the report are to outline ways in which changes to the building are tracked and ensure this HMP and associated action plans, implementation plans, and maintenance plans are regularly reviewed and updated.

#### 7.11.1 Monitoring

Regular monitoring of implementation of this HMP is required under the EPBC Regulations and will support the conservation of the heritage values of Snapper Island. Monitoring should involve the following activities:

• The condition of the heritage values will be monitored annually via physical inspections of Snapper Island using the cyclical maintenance checklists provided in this HMP. The condition assessment will be informed by the baseline condition provided in this HMP. The aim is to prevent further deterioration of the condition of the heritage values of Snapper Island; and

• Prompt action will be taken to address issues that are impacting the condition of the heritage values.

• Annual review of progress against the implementation plan at Section 8.

**Policy 31**

The implementation of this HMP and the condition of the heritage values of Snapper Island will be monitored annually.

#### 7.11.2 Recording

Where buildings, structures or landscape elements of heritage value are approved for demolition or removal, archival recording should be prepared. If the asset is of moderate, or high heritage value, a comprehensive archival recording should be undertaken. This would involve the production of photographic capture and plans, and measured drawings.

Future recordings would build on those previously produced by ERM in 2015 (updated in 2019) and 2021 and are to be prepared in accordance with the NSW State Government Guideline Photographic Recording of using Film or Digital Capture (2006) with copies lodged with the State Library of NSW.

**Policy 32**

Prepare a revised Archival Recording of the site or its affected areas prior to commencing any major changes or works to areas or items with heritage values. Details of the alteration works should be recorded following their completion.

#### 7.11.3 Reporting

Planned and unscheduled maintenance works and alterations to heritage items and areas need to be recorded by the PSP and Finance to provide a centralised record of changes to the place over time, and to satisfy Finance’s heritage register requirements under the EPBC Act. Records of all repairs and works, associated decisions and reasons for decisions will be kept for reporting and auditing purposes.

Electronic recording should capture any maintenance undertaken at Snapper Island with attention to significant fabric (refer to Figure Figure 5‑1 for significance of site elements). These records are to be incorporated in future updates of the statutory HMP.

The findings of the annual implementation review will be reported to Senior Leadership at Finance (i.e. Branch Head, Property Management and Projects). If progress does not align with the timeframes in the implementation plan, actions will be taken to address the outstanding actions.

In the event of divestment, a copy of site works records should be made available to the new site manager, and a record should continue to be maintained for the site.

**Policy 33**

Ensure routine maintenance as well as substantial maintenance to heritage items and areas at Snapper Island are recorded.

**Policy 34**

Records of substantial maintenance works and alterations are to be incorporated in future updates of the statutory HMP.

**Policy 35**

The implementation of this HMP will be reported annually to Senior Leadership.

#### 7.11.4 Review of the HMP

For this HMP to remain a useful heritage management and conservation tool it needs to be reviewed and updated at regular intervals. The review is to be undertaken by Finance, with assistance from a heritage specialist as required. The review is to focus on amending responsibilities, include commentary on any works undertaken since the last HMP review; it will also consider any changes or planned changes of use and the interpretation strategy.

Whilst Snapper Island remains a Commonwealth property, in accordance with the EPBC Act, this HMP must be reviewed at least once every five years. The review should assess the content of the plan and determine its effectiveness in protecting the heritage values of the place and make recommendations for updating or re-writing the plan as required.

Finance/PSP will check the implementation of this HMP, including actions identified in Table 7‑1 and cyclical maintenance schedule through Finance’s annual Property Asset Management Plan (PAMP) process, with corrective actions to be recorded and implemented within agreed timeframes.

**Policy 36**

This HMP should be reviewed and updated at least once every five years in accordance with Section 341X of the EPBC Act. Finance/PSP will check the implementation of this HMP through the annual PAMP process.

### 7.12 Maintenance of Heritage Significance

This section provides maintenance protocols to guide regular inspections, Catch up Maintenance Schedule, Cyclical Preventative Maintenance Inspection Schedule and Planned Maintenance Works that ensures heritage values are conserved.

The Site has not been subject to regular, effective maintenance, which has contributed to its poor condition overall.

#### 7.12.1 Reasons for Maintenance

The desirable standard of maintenance depends on the intensity of use and climatic conditions. A policy of preventative maintenance work is less expensive in every way. Neglecting maintenance leads to decay and may lead to costly repair or the building replacement cycle being the only viable option.

A clear understanding of the building materials, construction, and its development, and as a result the likely problems that may be encountered, assists with the anticipation and appropriate maintenance and repair. Maintenance is defined by the Burra Charter as the continuous protective care of the fabric, contents and setting of a place. Preventative Maintenance is carried out to prevent an item failing or wearing out by providing systematic inspection, detection and prevention of damage or failure, and is usually programmed. If carried out properly, preventative maintenance will reduce the probability of decay. This contrasts with corrective maintenance, whereby damaged or deteriorating material is replaced or repaired. Preventative maintenance can not only reduce, or even avoid, the need for repairs later; it will prevent the loss of original fabric and is cost-effective. Corrective maintenance is disruptive and costly in both significant fabric and financial terms.

Regular inspections and preventative maintenance thus form the basis of the most cost-effective approach to the conservation of buildings and items of heritage significance. While some heritage buildings and items have materials that may require specialised care, the majority of preventative maintenance is standard housekeeping that can be undertaken as part of programmed maintenance activities for all assets.

Regular inspection and preventative maintenance activities can contribute to extending the life of an asset and reduce the whole of life costs associated with using the building. The need for major conservation work is most commonly the result of insufficient inspection and maintenance. The cost benefits of developing and implementing an inspection and preventative maintenance regime is outlined in the graph below (Figure 7‑1).

Continuation of existing uses and seeking new or adaptive re-use of heritage items also plays a key role in conserving heritage values by ensuring maintenance issues are identified early and supporting justification for funding works for ongoing care.

Figure 7‑1 Preventative Maintenance Costs Diagram (Source: NSW Heritage Office 2004:3)

[image: A two axis graph showing repair costs arrow on the vertical and time in years arrow on the horizontal.]

#### 7.12.2 Regular Inspection

Regular annual inspections should be undertaken by the landowner/manager at Snapper Island. These inspections are important as part of the ongoing care of the heritage values and fabric of the buildings and the associated elements on the island. Additional guidance for these inspections is provided below.

Maintenance and examination of the Site should ideally be tackled by routines of six monthly, annual and five yearly inspections, followed by brief reports. The inspections should be undertaken by the Property Manager and a heritage practitioner with knowledge of historic buildings and conservation of building materials and methods. These should be integrated into annual inspections.

Examination of the fabric of the buildings and their surrounds should be conducted systematically by circulating around each building externally and internally in a clockwise direction. Examiners should use their senses to observe and note each building and surrounding environment condition and defects.

The checklists provided in Section 7.12.3 outline what needs to be considered for Snapper Island to ensure issues that may have an impact on heritage values are mitigated. This checklist should also be integrated into the owner’s inspection checklist.

#### 7.12.3 Maintenance Plans

This section presents prioritised implementation plans comprising specific works tasks to manage the heritage values of the Site and individually significant features. These works should be integrated into the Finance and PSP maintenance plans, minor capital expenditure programs or capital expenditure programs.

The maintenance plans are prioritised according to the risk framework and divided into:

• Catch Up Maintenance;

• Cyclical Preventative Maintenance Schedule; and

• Planned Works.

##### 7.12.3.1 Catch Up Maintenance

A schedule of catch-up maintenance and repairs is provided below, including general housekeeping and maintenance issues. Buildings that have been assessed as structurally unsound (Macquarie 2023) have ‘make safe’ actions listed in the event that these buildings are retained into the short and medium term. The repairs are prioritised under three categories:

• High (complete within 6 months) – work considered essential for the safety of the site or the users and visitors;

• Medium (complete within 12 months) – recommended work of lower priority but necessary to minimise long- term damage to the site’s fabric and to enable the continued use of the site; and

• Low (Complete within 2 years) – work necessary to maintain the condition of the place and its heritage values.

Provided the maintenance outlined in Table 7‑1 is undertaken in accordance with Section 7.12 by a tradesperson familiar with heritage conservation, further heritage advice is not warranted. These actions should only be undertaken if it has been determined safe to do so by Finance with input from safety risk advisor where necessary.

##### 7.12.3.2 Cyclical Preventative Maintenance Schedule

The following checklist is an indication of what needs to be considered for the Site to ensure issues that may have an impact on heritage values are mitigated. The following should also be integrated into the owner’s inspection checklist.

##### 7.12.3.3 Six-Month Cyclical Maintenance Plan

• check plant growth over island and prune/mow where necessary;

• remove vegetation build-up from around edges of buildings;

• inspect Cabbage Trees for damage or die-back;

• monitor animal/vermin activity and place additional traps/baits where necessary;

• check all bird barriers for damage and replace where necessary;

• inspect termite activity and treat where necessary;

• check external windows and doors for damage and repair as necessary;

• check for graffiti/other signs that the island has been illegally accessed;

• check that roofs are watertight, and patch and repair where necessary with compatible materials;

• check rainwater goods to ensure they are free of leaves and other debris; and

• conduct basic cleaning to ensure condition does not continue to deteriorate.

##### 7.12.3.4 Annual Cyclical Maintenance Plan

• remove rubbish or unwanted material and clean the interiors of each building;

• check opening and closing of all doors and windows and ease and lubricate as required;

• check the condition of stairs, seawalls and retaining walls;

• check condition of all directional and interpretive signage (where available);

• check condition and integrity of all contributory elements of significance identified in Table 5‑4;

• inspect roofs (outside and inside);

• check and clear gutters, downpipes, stormwater pits and drains;

• apply rust treatment to all external metal elements and repaint as required;

• repair any broken glazing, reapply putty to loose panes, and clean windows and painted surrounds;

• repair and repaint any damaged timber elements;

• replace any damaged interior cladding sheets;

• clean light fittings and change bulbs or fluorescent tubes;

• horticulturalist to inspect health and condition of Cabbage Tree; and

• check fire extinguishers and replace as required.

##### 7.12.3.5 5 Yearly Cyclical Maintenance Plan

Every five years the heritage practitioner should make a full report especially noting any structural defects that should be kept under observation and the cyclical maintenance plan should be updated, drawing attention to any defects that should be kept under observation or where further study might be required. Proposed actions should be prioritised and divided into the following categories and recorded in a log book:

• small items (basically good housekeeping);

• should building upgrades occur, requirement for repairs to mechanical, electrical, plumbing and drainage services;

• a rolling program of long-term preventive maintenance carried out year by year; and

• major items of conservation such as roofing, walls, windows, doors and floor coverings and services.

The five yearly inspections should form part of the five yearly HMP review. The scope of a typical five yearly inspection would be consistent with the list of items set out in both the six monthly and the annual inspection.

| High | Adopt this HMP. | Finance | At completion of final HMP. | Section 7.1<br>Policy 1 |

Maintenance actions do not require further heritage advice (e.g. HIA) provided they are undertaken in accordance with Section 7.12 of this HMP if it is deemed safe and practicable to do so.</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="7"><b>Future Use Options:</b><br>The Guard House is the natural entry point onto Snapper Island. Depending on future use of the rest of the island it could be used as a ticket office, administration building, introductory interpretation display, or café booth.</td>
</tr>

</tr>

<td colspan="8"><b>Routine Maintenance Guidance:</b><br>• Check roofs are watertight, patch and repair as necessary with compatible materials<br>• Check gutters and downpipes are free flowing, remove any blockages<br>• Ease doors and windows and lubricate metal fittings, rehang if necessary<br>• Remove unwanted material from interior and clean<br>• Apply mould treatment to interior walls and furniture<br>• Apply rust treatment to galvanised corrugated iron and metal fittings and fixtures<br>• Clean out and repair/replace gutters and downpipes<br>• Repair any broken window glass<br>• Repair and repaint any damaged timber elements<br>• Repaint galvanised corrugated iron and steel balustrades as needed<br><b>N.B. Maintenance actions do not require further heritage advice provided they are undertaken in accordance with Section 7.12 of this HMP if it is deemed safe and practicable to do so.</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Future Use Options:</b><br>The Officers Wardroom Mess has heritage values linked to its recreational use by the naval training officers. Depending on future use of the rest of the island it would be ideally placed to be used as a bar, café, or hostel kitchen.</td>
</tr>

<td colspan="9"><b>Condition:</b><br>The 2023 structural assessment has determined that this building is extensively deteriorated from fungal decay damage (commonly referred to as rotting) due to exposure to moisture. The structure is not performing as intended, is not deemed fit for purpose and is not feasibly recoverable due to the extent of damage to the timber roof and wall frames also floor.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="9"><b>Integrity:</b> Low<br>The majority of the timber framing and the door are likely original, while the corrugated iron cladding has probably been replaced. The roofing with some timber supports has been recently replaced.</td>
</tr>

<td colspan="9"><b>Tolerance for Change:</b> High<br>Structural repairs are not considered practically achievable and actions to mitigate risk of injury as part of make safe works are presented in the catch up maintenance and repairs section below, if it is deemed safe and practicable to do so.<br>Subject to a HIA demolition is considered an option for this building given its poor condition and low heritage significance. Should a HIA process determine the building is to be retained, restored or reconstructed, general recommendations regarding tolerance for change are made in Table 6‑3 and specific maintenance recommendations are provided below.<br>If the building is to be retained, restored or reconstructed, the following features should be repaired and retained where possible, or replaced with like-for-like if necessary:<br>• Timber framing<br>• Timber fascia<br>• Timber slat door with metal hinges<br>The following features may be replaced with like-for-like material:<br>• Galvanised corrugated iron cladding or roofing<br>• Pebble-dash concrete paths<br><b>N.B. If the building is to be retained or restored or reconstructed, repair or replacement with like-for-like should be undertaken by a tradesperson familiar with heritage conservation. The scope of works should refer to Section 6.10 of this HMP for guidance; all changes must be documented in accordance with Policies 26 and 27. Heritage advice can also be sought for confirmation of the scope of these works.</b><br>The following features may be removed and/or replaced to be more sympathetic to the heritage values of the building:<br>• Door bolt and lock<br>• Metal fascia<br>• Electrical lighting and fittings<br>The following may be installed provided it is undertaken in a manner that is sympathetic to the heritage values of the building:<br>• Additional lighting<br>• Security features<br>• Reversible divisions or fixed furniture<br><b>N.B. Heritage advice in the form of a HIA is likely to be required for removal or replacement of these fixed features or installation of any new features. Less extensive changes could be managed by a tradesperson familiar with heritage conservation and reference to Section 6.10 of this HMP for guidance; all changes must be documented in according with Policies 26 and 27.</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="9"><b>Routine Maintenance Guidance:</b><br>• Check roofs are watertight, patch and repair as necessary with compatible materials<br>• Check gutters and downpipes are free flowing, remove any blockages<br>• Remove unwanted material from interior and clean<br>• Apply rust treatment to galvanised corrugated iron and metal fittings and fixtures<br>• Repair and repaint any damaged timber elements<br>• Repaint galvanised corrugated iron and steel balustrades as needed<br><b>N.B. Maintenance actions do not require further heritage advice (e.g. HIA) provided they are undertaken in accordance with Section 7.12 of this HMP, if it is deemed safe and practicable to do so.</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="9"><b>Future Use Options:</b><br>The Storage Room has limited options for use due to its utilitarian construction and being open to the weather. It would suit continued use as a storage room for maintenance equipment or could be an open display area for future activities on the island.</td>
</tr>

<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Description of building/item:</b><br>The Boatshed and Workshop are located on the port side of the island, to the north of the guardhouse. The Boatshed/workshop are timber framed structures with corrugated iron sheeting on the exterior. The Boatshed is open at the northern end, the workshop has a small opening at the western end. Leading from the Boatshed is a ramp constructed of timber and iron ‘I’ beams.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Condition:</b><br>The timber framed structure is extensively deteriorated from fungal decay damage (commonly referred to as rotting) due to exposure to seawater from tidal inflow and weather exposure. Significant soil erosion has occurred from tidal inflow causing subsidence and collapse of the concrete and timber floors and severe damage to piers supporting the structure. The structure is not performing as intended, is not deemed fit for purpose and is not feasibly recoverable due to the extent of severe damage to the structural elements.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Integrity:</b> Low<br>Parts of the timber framing may be original, but much is likely to have been replaced along with the corrugated iron. The steel elements are probably original.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Tolerance for Change:</b> High<br>Structural repairs are not considered practically achievable and actions to mitigate risk of injury as part of make safe works are presented in the catch up maintenance and repairs section below, if it is deemed safe and practicable to do so.<br>Subject to a HIA, demolition is considered an option for this building given its extremely poor condition. Should a HIA process determine the building be retained, restored or reconstructed general recommendations regarding tolerance for change are made in Table 6‑3 and specific maintenance recommendations are provided below.<br>If the building is to be retained, restored or reconstructed, the following features should be retained:<br>• Function<br>• Basic form<br>• Use of timber and galvanized corrugated iron<br>The following may be installed provided it is undertaken in a manner that is sympathetic to the heritage values of the building:<br>• Reinstallation of masts or flagpoles at front and rear<br><b>N.B. Heritage advice in the form of a HIA is likely to be required for removal or replacement of these fixed features or installation of any new features. Less extensive changes could be managed by a tradesperson familiar with heritage conservation and reference to Section 6.10 of this HMP for guidance; all changes must be documented in according with Policies 26 and 27.</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Routine Maintenance Guidance:</b><br>• Check roofs are watertight, patch and repair as necessary with compatible materials<br>• Check gutters and downpipes are free flowing, remove any blockages<br>• Remove vegetation from around exterior<br>• Remove unwanted material from interior where safe and accessible<br>• Apply rust treatment to galvanised corrugated iron<br>• Repaint remaining galvanised corrugated iron as needed<br><b>N.B. Maintenance actions do not require further heritage advice (e.g. HIA) provided they are undertaken in accordance with Section 7.12 of this HMP, if it is deemed safe and practicable to do so.</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Future Use Options:</b><br>The Boatshed has heritage values linked to its maritime use by the naval training cadets. As it is expected that some form of maritime activities will be a part of the future use of Snapper Island, a restored or reconstructed boatshed for temporary access or permanent storage would be the primary recommendation for a building at this location. Should a non-maritime future use be determined, and should the existing building be demolished and reconstructed, then its design should be representative of its former use but adapted to suit its future use.</td>
</tr>

### A.6 Asset No. 8 – Gymnasium

<table>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>Asset Name:</b> Gymnasium</td>
<td colspan="4"><b>Asset No:</b> 8</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="5">[image: The photo of the wall of Gymnasium facing the Cockatoo Island on a Forecastle Deck . The building is made from corrugated metal sheets , currently severely rusted with 10 small windows and a corrugated roof, situated on a grassy area near water. The structure shows significant weathering and decay, with patches of rust covering most of its surface. The wall has the remains of the painted sign "SYDNEY TRAINING DEPOT' severely faded and barely readable.]</td>
<td colspan="3">[image: The photo of other wall of Gymnasium facing Building 9 (J.Payne Memorial Building) which is hidden from water explosion. The wall is made from corrugated metal sheet , currently severely rusted with 8 small windows captured on the photo The roof between covering area between Building 8 and 9 is damaged and sunlight is coming through the missing parts of the corrugated metal roof. The structure shows weathering and decay, with patches of rust . The small timber paint and other timber debris are stored next to the external wall of Gymnasium]</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="5">[image: Interior of a timber framed building with metal cladding and loose timbers on the floor. The metal is corroded.]</td>
<td colspan="3">[image: A photo of Gymnasium interior showing rusted machinery and scattered debris on a dirt floor. Natural light enters through broken windows, highlighting decay.]</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8">[image: A site plan map of Snapper Island in Sydney Harbour shows heritage management details with numbered site features marked in red circles. The plan includes a legend with symbols for site features, demolition, retaining walls, and water.]</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Current Use:</b> None</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="6"><b>CHL No:</b> 05477<br><b>RNE No:</b> 102559</td>
<td colspan="2"><b>AHDB Link for CHL Citation:</b> Snapper Island Commonwealth Heritage Listed Place ID 105477<br><b>Other heritage citations:</b> Snapper Island Register of the National Estate ID 102559</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="6"><b>Original Construction Date:</b> 1942</td>
<td colspan="2"><b>Original Use:</b> Gymnasium</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Heritage Value:</b> Moderate</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Significant Elements:</b><br>• Location and form<br>• Steel and galvanized corrugated iron construction<br>• ‘Sydney Training Depot’ lettering on external elevation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Description of building/item:</b><br>The Gymnasium is a single storey timber and corrugated iron structure located just north of the J. Payne Memorial Building at the fore end of the island. Timber and corrugated iron is currently being stored in between the two buildings. The interior consists of one long room with no internal divisions.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Condition:</b><br>The timber framed floor structure is extensively deteriorated from fungal decay damage (commonly referred to as rotting) due to exposure to seawater from tidal inflow and weather exposure. Significant soil erosion has occurred from tidal inflow causing subsidence and collapse of the timber floors and severe damage to piers supporting the structure.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Integrity:</b> Moderate<br>The steel framing is likely original, and some sheets of the corrugated iron could be original or at least early replacements. The remainder of the corrugated iron cladding and roofing and rainwater goods have been replaced. The doors and window frames appear original, as do the timber floorboards, although other parts of the floor have been replaced with pressed timber sheets.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Tolerance for Change:</b> Moderate<br>The 2023 structural assessment determined that the structure is not performing as intended, is not deemed fit for purpose and is not feasibly recoverable due to the extent of severe damage to the structural elements. Through a HIA, explore feasible and prudent options to determine whether the building could be retained.<br>Should the HIA determine that the building can be feasibly and prudently retained or reconstructed general recommendations regarding tolerance for change are made in Table 6‑3 and specific maintenance recommendations are provided below, if it is deemed safe and practicable to do so.<br>The following features should be repaired and retained where possible, or replaced with like-for-like if necessary:<br>• Steel framing<br>• Steel fenestration<br>• Timber door frame<br>• ‘Sydney Training Depot’ lettering on external elevation<br>The following features may be replaced with like-for-like material:<br>• Galvanised corrugated iron cladding or roofing<br>• Galvanised iron door awning<br>• Galvanised iron guttering and downpipes<br>• Timber board flooring<br>• Timber double doors with metal T-hinges<br><b>N.B. Repair or replacement with like-for-like should be undertaken by a tradesperson familiar with heritage conservation. The scope of works should refer to Section 6.10 of this HMP for guidance; all changes must be documented in accordance with Policies 26 and 27. Heritage advice can also be sought for confirmation of the scope of these works.</b><br>The following features may be removed and/or replaced to be more sympathetic to the heritage values of the building:<br>• External signage<br>• Above water, timber walkway around three sides of building<br>• Timber sheet flooring<br>• Steel safety rails/balustrades<br>• Aluminium and corrugated polycarbonate sheeting windows<br>• Security mesh between walls and roof<br>• Electrical lighting and fittings<br>• Moveable items<br>The following may be installed provided it is undertaken in a manner that is sympathetic to the heritage values of the building:<br>• Additional lighting<br>• Security features<br>• Reversible divisions or fixed furniture<br><b>N.B. Heritage advice in the form of a HIA is likely to be required for removal or replacement of these fixed features or installation of any new features. Less extensive changes could be managed by a tradesperson familiar with heritage conservation and reference to Section 6.10 of this HMP for guidance; all changes must be documented in according with Policies 26 and 27.</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Routine Maintenance Guidance:</b><br>• Check roofs are watertight, patch and repair as necessary with compatible materials<br>• Check gutters and downpipes are free flowing, remove any blockages<br>• Ease doors and windows and lubricate metal fittings, rehang if necessary<br>• Remove unwanted material from interior and clean<br>• Apply rust treatment to galvanised corrugated iron and metal fittings and fixtures<br>• Clean out and repair/replace gutters and downpipes<br>• Repair any broken window glass<br>• Repair and repaint doors as needed<br>• Repaint remaining galvanised corrugated iron as needed, leaving/repainting ‘Sydney Training Depot’<br><b>N.B. Maintenance actions do not require further heritage advice (e.g. HIA) provided they are undertaken in accordance with Section 7.12 of this HMP, if it is deemed safe and practicable to do so.</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Future Use Options:</b><br>The large open space of the Gymnasium makes it an ideal candidate for virtually any type of adaptive reuse. It could be reinstituted as a gymnasium or physical training facility or be used for new use activities such as carpentry or metal work. It could also be used as a function or dining space, or an exhibition space.</td>
</tr>

### A.7 Asset No. 9 – J. Payne Memorial Building

<table>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>Asset Name:</b> J. Payne Memorial Building</td>
<td colspan="4"><b>Asset No:</b> 9</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="5">[image: The photo of Building 9 (J. Payne Memorial Building) from the elevation. capturing the first floor and the ground floor at the bottom. <td colspan="3">[image: A photo of side of exterior (two walls) of J. Payne Memorial Building from the lower level and timber walkways near walls. The building is made from corrugated metal sheets, most of them are light green colour. There are one rectangular shape window on the lower level and same of the upper level.]</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="5">[image: Photo of Building 9 (J.Payne Memorial Building) interior, picturing the timber flooring and structural framing painted white with metal bunk beds frames against the left and north wall.]</td>
