</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8">[image: A site plan map of Snapper Island in Sydney Harbour shows heritage management details with numbered site features marked in red circles. The plan includes a legend with symbols for site features, demolition, retaining walls, and water.]</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Current Use:</b> None</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="6"><b>CHL No:</b> 05477<br><b>RNE No:</b> 102559</td>
<td colspan="2"><b>AHDB Link for CHL Citation:</b> Snapper Island Commonwealth Heritage Listed Place ID 105477<br><b>Other heritage citations:</b> Snapper Island Register of the National Estate ID 102559</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="6"><b>Original Construction Date:</b> 1938</td>
<td colspan="2"><b>Original Use:</b> Accommodation and Recreation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Heritage Value:</b> Moderate</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Significant Elements:</b><br>• Location and form<br>• Accommodation and recreation function<br>• Timber and galvanised corrugated iron construction<br>• Internal signs and furniture that relate to cadet use of building</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Description of building/item:</b><br>The J. Payne Memorial building is a two-storey timber frame corrugated iron structure situated on the lower deck at the fore end of the island. The structure contains bunks and storage rooms on the lower floor and a former recreation room on the upper floor. The upper floor has a double door on the aft side, which formerly lead to a walkway, which accessed the upper deck.<br>The building has timber floors throughout and the internal stairs have been removed and replaced with an aluminium ladder. The walls are clad with plasterboard and timber framing, with timber doors and window frames throughout.<br>The lower floor has two sets of double doors leading to a covered walkway between the J. Payne building and the Gymnasium, on the aft side. The doors lead into a main bunk room which leads into sea series of smaller rooms, including an officer’s quarters, a storage/locker room, a small sick bay in which the flooring has collapsed, and a sitting room.<br>The upper floor has a similar layout, featuring a central room with four smaller rooms branching off at the starboard and port ends. One of the rooms at the starboard end is a small kitchenette.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Condition:</b><br>The timber framed floor structure is extensively deteriorated from fungal decay damage (commonly referred to as rotting) due to exposure to seawater from tidal inflow and weather exposure. Significant soil erosion has occurred from tidal inflow causing subsidence and collapse of the timber floors and severe damage to piers supporting the structure.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Integrity:</b> High<br>Much of the original fabric appears relatively intact, although it is likely the corrugated and ribbed iron has been replaced along with the rainwater goods.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Tolerance for Change:</b> Moderate<br>The 2023 structural assessment determined that the structure is not performing as intended, is not deemed fit for purpose and is not feasibly recoverable due to the extent of severe damage to the structural elements. Through a HIA explore feasible and prudent options to determine whether the building could be retained.<br>Should a HIA determine the building be retained or reconstructed, general recommendations regarding tolerance for change are made in Table 6‑3 and specific maintenance recommendations are provided below, if it is deemed safe and practicable to do so.<br>The following features should be repaired and retained where possible, or replaced with like-for-like if necessary:<br>• Timber framing<br>• Timber and steel support posts<br>• Timber flooring<br>• VJ timber walls and ceiling<br>• Timber fenestration including sills<br>• Window glass<br>• Louvre windows<br>• Timber door frames<br>• All doors and any original handles and locks<br>• Timber kitchen hatch<br>• Timber skirting boards<br>• Internal timber, steel, and wire balustrade<br>• Fixed timber shelving<br>• Speaker system<br>• Fixed bed and navigation bench<br>• Bunk beds<br>• Timber lockers<br>• Fridge (if no safety issues)<br>• Pool table and equipment (until adaptive reuse in place)<br>• Maps and framed prints<br>• Internal signs that relate to cadet use of building<br>The following features may be replaced with like-for-like material:<br>• Galvanised corrugated iron cladding or roofing<br>• Galvanised iron fascias<br>• Galvanised iron guttering and downpipes<br>• Particle board internal cladding<br><b>N.B. Repair or replacement with like-for-like should be undertaken by a tradesperson familiar with heritage conservation. The scope of works should refer to Section 6.10 of this HMP for guidance; all changes must be documented in accordance with Policies 26 and 27. Heritage advice can also be sought for confirmation of the scope of these works.</b><br>The following features may be removed and/or replaced to be more sympathetic to the heritage values of the building:<br>• Ribbed corrugated metal roofing<br>• Safety fencing and external balustrades<br>• Security mesh on windows and between walls and roof<br>• Noticeboards<br>• Door bolts<br>• Curtains<br>• Kitchen fit-out<br>• Lighting and other electrical fittings<br>• Fire-fighting equipment and signage<br>• Any other moveable items not mentioned above<br>The following may be installed provided it is undertaken in a manner that is sympathetic to the heritage values of the building:<br>• Restoration of bridge to upper storey<br>• New internal stairway<br>• New kitchen windows<br>• Additional lighting<br>• Security features<br>• Reversible divisions or fixed furniture<br><b>N.B. Heritage advice in the form of a HIA is likely to be required for removal or replacement of these fixed features or installation of any new features. Less extensive changes could be managed by a tradesperson familiar with heritage conservation and reference to Section 6.10 of this HMP for guidance; all changes must be documented in according with Policies 26 and 27.</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Routine Maintenance Guidance:</b><br>• Check roofs are watertight, patch and repair as necessary with compatible materials<br>• Check gutters and downpipes are free flowing, remove any blockages<br>• Ease doors and windows and lubricate metal fittings, rehang if necessary<br>• Remove unwanted material from interior and clean<br>• Apply rust treatment to galvanised corrugated iron and metal fittings and fixtures<br>• Clean out and repair/replace gutters and downpipes<br>• Repair any broken window glass<br>• Repair or replace any damaged bird barriers<br>• Repair and repaint any damaged timber elements as needed<br>• Replace interior cladding sheets as needed<br>• Repaint steel elements as needed<br>• Repaint galvanised corrugated iron walls as needed<br><b>N.B. Maintenance actions do not require further heritage advice (e.g. HIA) provided they are undertaken in accordance with Section 7.12 of this HMP, if it is deemed safe and practicable to do so.</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Future Use Options:</b><br>The J. Payne Memorial Building has heritage values linked to its accommodation and recreational use by the naval cadets. Depending on future use of the rest of the island it would be ideally placed to be used for accommodation and related activities. This could be part of something like a hostel or a conference or function facility. Adaptive reuse for other purposes is also possible, with the rooms fairly easily converted into offices or working spaces.</td>
</tr>
<tr>

### A.8 Asset No. 10 – Main Deck, Museum

<td colspan="7"><b>Current Use:</b> None (storage)</td>

<tr>
<td colspan="5"><b>CHL No:</b> 05477<br><b>RNE No:</b> 102559</td>
<td colspan="2"><b>AHDB Link for CHL Citation:</b> Snapper Island Commonwealth Heritage Listed Place ID 105477<br><b>Other heritage citations:</b> Snapper Island Register of the National Estate ID 102559</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="5"><b>Original Construction Date:</b> 1928 at naval depot at Drummoyne and brought to Snapper Island in 1932</td>
<td colspan="2"><b>Original Use:</b> Training Hall</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="7"><b>Heritage Value:</b> High</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="7"><b>Significant Elements:</b><br>• Location and form<br>• Timber and galvanised corrugated iron construction<br>• Nautical features such as skirting boards across door frames and painted features on floor<br>• Engine room<br>• Foundation stone and plaque<br>• Moveable heritage items and signs that relate to cadet use of building</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="7"><b>Description of building/item:</b><br>The Main Deck is the most prominent building on Snapper Island, as it is centrally located and occupies the majority of the upper deck. The structure is single storey timber frame with corrugated iron cladding to the exterior. The roof is hipped with skillion surrounds; all clad with corrugated iron sheeting. The main entrance is through a set of double timber doors on the port side of the building, leading into the main hall of the deck. There are secondary entrances on the starboard and fore sides of the building, leading to the kitchen. There are also two fire exit doors on the fore and aft sides, although these have been permanently fixed closed since the abandonment of the building.<br>The interior of the structure is simple in design. There are 15 rooms branching off the main hall, some with remnant bulkhead fittings at the base of the entry. These were designed to simulate stepping over the partitions on a naval ship. The walls are clad with vertical timber boards which have been painted in pale blue. The roof framing and cladding is exposed with no remaining ceiling. The floors are timber boards, with some accent paintings in hallways and surrounding doors.<br>The aft end of the interior retains the paintings used to decorate the HMAS Sydney display area. No fittings remain in this location.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="7"><b>Condition:</b><br>The structural assessment has determined that this building is serviceable although has some serious and urgent issues of concern, particularly regarding replacing missing roof cladding to protect the structure.<br>The structure is aged and deteriorated, however it is performing as intended and is deemed fit for purpose and recoverable. There is significant dust, grime, and bird droppings throughout. Water damage and peeling paint is evident in many rooms. The concrete flooring in the kitchen has significant cracking, and carpets in other rooms need replacement.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="7"><b>Integrity:</b> High<br>While substantial change was made to the building when relocated to Snapper Island, much of the fabric original to that point in time appears intact, with the exception of the corrugated iron cladding and roofing and rainwater goods. At least three external doors have been replaced. Electrical lighting and the kitchen fit-out have also been installed. They vinyl flooring and carpets are also not original.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="7"><b>Tolerance for Change:</b> Low<br>General recommendations regarding tolerance for change are made in Table 6‑3.<br>The following features should be repaired and retained where possible, or replaced with like-for-like if necessary:<br>• Timber framing<br>• Timber fascias<br>• Brick foundation including ventilation bricks<br>• Foundation stone and plaque<br>• External hooks<br>• Speaker system<br>• Timber door frames<br>• All non-particle board doors and any original handles and locks<br>• Timber fenestration including sills – external and internal<br>• Window glass and fittings<br>• Early external and internal light fittings<br>• Timber flooring<br>• Internal wall cladding and ceilings (where asbestos, replace with fibre cement)<br>• VJ timber walls and ceilings<br>• Painted designs on internal walls and floor<br>• Timber skirting boards<br>• Internal steel post<br>• Engine and associated fixtures and signage<br>• Fixed timber shelving and bunk<br>• Hinged wall tables<br>• Rubble drip-catcher<br>• Internal signs that relate to cadet use of building<br>• Items noted in moveable heritage inventory<br>The following features may be replaced with like-for-like material:<br>• Timber decking and posts<br>• Timber walkway and balustrade to Building 9<br>• Galvanised corrugated iron cladding or roofing<br>• Galvanised iron guttering and downpipes<br>• Flashing<br>• Concrete apron with brick edging<br>• Concrete steps with steel balustrades<br><b>N.B. Repair or replacement with like-for-like should be undertaken by a tradesperson familiar with heritage conservation. The scope of works should refer to Section 6.10 of this HMP for guidance; all changes must be documented in accordance with Policies 26 and 27. Heritage advice can also be sought for confirmation of the scope of these works.</b><br>The following features may be removed and/or replaced to be more sympathetic to the heritage values of the building:<br>• Louvre windows<br>• Security mesh on windows<br>• Fire-fighting equipment and signage<br>• Aluminium internal windows<br>• Door bolts<br>• Modern lighting and other electrical fittings<br>• Non-fixed shelving<br>• Kitchen fit-out<br>• Vinyl flooring<br>• Fibre floor coverings<br>• Safety fencing and external balustrades<br>• Any other moveable items not mentioned in the moveable heritage inventory<br>• Trees and other vegetation (excluding two Cabbage Trees)<br>The following may be installed provided it is undertaken in a manner that is sympathetic to the heritage values of the building:<br>• Reinstallation of internal features such as balustrade and HMAS Sydney model and wheel<br>• Reinstallation of internal doors around main hall<br>• Reinstallation of mast or flagpole at front of building<br>• Reinstall steel and wire fencing at front to each side of the concrete stairway<br>• Reinstallation of rifle range<br>• Additional lighting<br>• Security features<br>• Reversible divisions or fixed furniture<br><b>N.B. Heritage advice in the form of a HIA is likely to be required for removal or replacement of these fixed features or installation of any new features. Less extensive changes could be managed by a tradesperson familiar with heritage conservation and reference to Section 6.10 of this HMP for guidance; all changes must be documented in according with Policies 26 and 27.</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="7"><b>Routine Maintenance Guidance:</b><br>• Check roofs are watertight, patch and repair as necessary with compatible materials<br>• Check gutters and downpipes are free flowing, remove any blockages<br>• Ease doors and windows and lubricate metal fittings, rehang if necessary<br>• Remove unwanted material from interior and clean<br>• Remove vegetation from around foundations and replace any loose bricks<br>• Apply rust treatment to galvanised corrugated iron and metal fittings and fixtures<br>• Clean out and repair/replace gutters and downpipes<br>• Repair any broken window glass<br>• Repair or replace any damaged bird barriers<br>• Repair and repaint any damaged timber elements as needed<br>• Replace interior cladding sheets as needed<br>• Repaint galvanised corrugated iron walls as needed<br><b>N.B. Maintenance actions do not require further heritage advice (e.g. HIA) provided they are undertaken in accordance with Section 7.12 of this HMP, if it is deemed safe and practicable to do so.</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="7"><b>Future Use Options:</b><br>The Main Deck is the feature building on Snapper Island and future use should reflect this. It has both a training and exhibition history and both these uses would be ideal for future activities. It could also be converted into a café, shop, function room, or art gallery.</td>
</tr>
<tr>

</tr>

### A.9 Asset No. 14 – Officer’s Cottage

<td colspan="7"><b>CHL No:</b> 05477<br><b>RNE No:</b> 102559</td>

</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="7"><b>Original Construction Date:</b> 1932</td>
<td colspan="3"><b>Original Use:</b> Officer’s Cottage</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="10"><b>Heritage Value:</b> Moderate</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="10"><b>Significant Elements:</b><br>• Location and form<br>• Residential function<br>• Timber and galvanized corrugated iron construction</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="10"><b>Description of building/item:</b><br>The Officer’s Cottage is a small single storey corrugated iron structure, probably with a timber frame (although this is not visible). The roof is a corrugated iron skillion, The Cottage consists of two rooms, a living/dining/kitchen space and a bedroom, with an ensuite bathroom.<br>Access to the cottage is through a timber door into the living area, which contains a small kitchenette, a hearth/fireplace, and a sitting area. Timber floorboards are present throughout, except in the bathroom where carpet has been retained. The interior walls are timber cladding and plasterboard. The splashback to the kitchenette is linoleum or vinyl. The bathroom contains a toilet, small sink and medicine cabinet.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="10"><b>Condition:</b><br>The structural assessment has determined that this building is serviceable although has some non-urgent but serious issues of concern.<br>The structure is aged and deteriorated, however it performing as intended and is deemed fit for purpose and recoverable. The exterior of structure is in poor condition, with corrosion impacting the external walls. The condition of the roof is very poor, as the corrugated iron has detached and is allowing water and other materials into the structure. The interior is in fair condition, although the cabinets are water damaged and dirty. The bathroom is in fair condition, although it is unlikely that the plumbing is still in good working order.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="10"><b>Integrity:</b> Moderate<br>The timber framing, walls and floor are likely original, although the corrugated iron cladding has been replaced and the entire roof including timber supports is new. It is unclear whether the bedroom wall cladding is original, but the aluminium window is certainly not.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="10"><b>Tolerance for Change:</b> Moderate<br>General recommendations regarding tolerance for change are made in Table 6‑3.<br>The following features should be repaired and retained where possible, or replaced with like-for-like if necessary:<br>• Timber framing<br>• Timber fascias<br>• Timber fenestration including sills<br>• Window glass and fittings<br>• External timber door with original handle and lock<br>• Timber door frames<br>• Timber flooring<br>• Timber internal cladding<br>• Fixed timber shelving<br>• Concrete base of stove alcove<br>• Concrete foundations<br>The following features may be replaced with like-for-like material:<br>• Galvanised corrugated iron cladding or roofing<br>• Galvanised iron gutters and downpipes<br>• Flashing<br>• Timber and steel roof frame<br>• Timber decking<br>• Pebble-dash concrete paths<br><b>N.B. Repair or replacement with like-for-like should be undertaken by a tradesperson familiar with heritage conservation. The scope of works should refer to Section 6.10 of this HMP for guidance; all changes must be documented in accordance with Policies 26 and 27. Heritage advice can also be sought for confirmation of the scope of these works.</b><br>The following features may be removed and/or replaced to be more sympathetic to the heritage values of the building:<br>• Metal fascia<br>• Security mesh on windows<br>• Kitchenette and bathroom fit-out<br>• Internal cladding of stove alcove<br>• Aluminium windows<br>• Internal particle board wall cladding and ceilings<br>• Internal lighting and electrical fittings<br>The following may be installed provided it is undertaken in a manner that is sympathetic to the heritage values of the building:<br>• Reinstallation of timber ceilings<br>• Reinstallation of timber lattice near front door<br>• New kitchenette and bathroom fit-out<br>• Additional lighting<br>• Security features<br>• Reversible divisions or fixed furniture<br><b>N.B. Heritage advice in the form of a HIA is likely to be required for removal or replacement of these fixed features or installation of any new features. Less extensive changes could be managed by a tradesperson familiar with heritage conservation and reference to Section 6.10 of this HMP for guidance; all changes must be documented in according with Policies 26 and 27.</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="10"><b>Routine Maintenance Guidance:</b><br>• Check roofs are watertight, patch and repair as necessary with compatible materials<br>• Check gutters and downpipes are free flowing, remove any blockages<br>• Ease doors and windows and lubricate metal fittings, rehang if necessary<br>• Remove unwanted material from interior and clean<br>• Apply rust treatment to galvanised corrugated iron and metal fittings and fixtures<br>• Clean out and repair/replace gutters and downpipes<br>• Repair any broken window glass<br>• Repair and repaint any damaged timber elements as needed<br>• Replace interior cladding sheets as needed<br>• Repaint galvanised corrugated iron walls as needed<br><b>N.B. Maintenance actions do not require further heritage advice (e.g. HIA) provided they are undertaken in accordance with Section 7.12 of this HMP, if it is deemed safe and practicable to do so.</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="10"><b>Future Use Options:</b><br>The Officer’s Cottage has heritage values linked to its use as a residence and this function would be ideal to continue. Depending on future use of the island the residence could be permanent or temporary, e.g. a caretakers cottage, holiday rental, or an accommodation space for teachers or instructors. Adaptive reuse for other purposes is also possible, including an administration office, shop, or gallery.</td>
</tr>

<td colspan="8"><b>Description of building/item:</b><br>Sea walls constructed from blasting rubble, with additional rock from around the harbour. The sea walls have a total length of 85 m, 2.4 m high and 1.5 m in thickness and increased the area of the island by one-third. They shape the island into the form of a boat, with the walls being the bow and stern.</td>

<td colspan="8"><b>Condition:</b><br>Seawall construction is ad hoc and not in accordance with acceptable construction practices. The seawall structure is generally dilapidated, unstable, is not performing as intended, is not deemed fit for purpose and is not feasibly recoverable (apart from concrete section adjacent Building 2). The seawall has experienced extensive degradation and partial failure in localised sections resulting in erosion of the shoreline where seawall failure apparent, including southern, western, northern and eastern sides of the island. A section of reinforced concrete seawall located within approx. 15-20 m to north and south sides of Building 2 remains serviceable despite extensive fretting and degradation from wet / dry action of salt water. This wall section is feasibly recoverable. Structural repairs are not considered practically achievable (apart from concrete section adjacent Building 2) and actions to mitigate risk of injury as part of make safe works are presented in the catch up maintenance and repairs section below.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Integrity:</b> High<br>Little change appears to have been made to the sea walls since their construction. A section of reinforced concrete has been added to the north and south sides of Building 2.</td>

Appendix B Heritage Citation

**Australian Heritage Database Citation for Snapper Island, NSW Accessed 16 May 2025**

**Snapper Island, Drummoyne, NSW, Australia**

**Photographs:** None

**List:** Commonwealth Heritage List

**Class:** Historic

**Legal Status:** Listed place (15/07/2004)

**Place ID:** 105477

**Place File No:** 1/17/013/0009

**Summary Statement of Significance**

Snapper Island, comprising the original sandstone area, fore and aft areas of made ground, a range of utilitarian buildings and maritime structures, is historically important as the primary expression of the Navy League UK, established at Drummoyne in 1921 by Len Forsythe, who saw the need to establish a voluntary training scheme for young boys, as naval cadets. The Sea Cadet movement is believed to be one of the oldest youth movements in the English speaking world.

The Snapper Island facility was officially opened on 26 November 1932 by Sir Charles Cox, on behalf of the Minister for Defence, as a living memorial to HMAS Sydney. HMAS Sydney, the first Royal Australian Navy ship to sink an enemy warship, the Emden, had been an inspiration to Forsythe. Snapper Island was the headquarters of Sydney's naval cadet groups and the Navy League from 1932 to 1977, and as such provided a voluntary step towards recruitment in the Australian Navy. (Criterion A.4)

(Historic Themes: 6.3 Training people for the workplace, 7.7 Defending Australia, 8.9 Commemorating significant events)

Snapper Island is of exceptional interest as a privately initiated and seminal naval cadet training facility, as the earliest surviving Navy League training depot in NSW and for its ship-like planning and layout. (Criterion D.2)

The Snapper Island training facility is recognised for its contribution to the landscape values of Sydney Harbour. (Criterion E.1)

Snapper Island is highly valued by Sydney's naval cadet groups and the local communities for its symbolic, cultural, educational and social associations. (Criterion G.1)

Snapper Island is closely associated with Len Forsythe, founder of the Navy League in Australia, and the driving force behind the development of Snapper Island as the focus of naval cadet training in Sydney. (Criterion H.1)

**Official Values**

**Criterion A Processes**

Snapper Island, comprising the original sandstone area, fore and aft areas of made ground, a range of utilitarian buildings and maritime structures and the Len Forsythe Museum Collection, is historically important as the primary expression of the Navy League UK, established at Drummoyne in 1921 by Len Forsythe, who saw the need to establish a voluntary training scheme for young boys, as naval cadets. The Sea Cadet movement is believed to be one of the oldest youth movements in the English speaking world.

The Snapper Island facility was officially opened on 26 November 1932 by Sir Charles Cox, on behalf of the Minister for Defence, as a living memorial to HMAS Sydney. HMAS Sydney, the first Royal Australian Navy ship to sink an enemy warship, the Emden, had been an inspiration to Forsythe. Snapper Island was the headquarters of Sydney's naval cadet groups and the Navy League from 1932 to 1977, and as such provided a voluntary step towards recruitment in the Australian Navy.

**Attributes**

All buildings, structures, artefacts, retaining walls, landscaping wharves, ramps and other features on the island.

**Criterion D Characteristic values**

Snapper Island is of exceptional interest as a privately initiated and seminal naval cadet training facility, as the earliest surviving Navy League training depot in NSW and for its ship like planning and layout.

**Attributes**

All aspects of development on the island, including the shiplike planning and layout.

**Criterion E Aesthetic characteristics**

The Snapper Island training facility is recognised for its contribution to the landscape values of Sydney Harbour.

**Attributes**

All aspects of the place including built form, open space and relationship to the harbour.

**Criterion G Social value**

Snapper Island is highly valued by Sydney's naval cadet groups and the local communities for its symbolic, cultural, educational and social associations.

**Attributes**

All aspects of the place.

**Criterion H Significant people**

Snapper Island is closely associated with Len Forsythe, founder of the Navy League in Australia, and the driving force behind the development of Snapper Island as the focus of naval cadet training in Sydney.

**Attributes**

Planning and layout of the facility.

**Description**

Snapper Island is a 1.65-hectare island at the mouth of the Parramatta River located approximately 300 metres north-east of Birkenhead Point, Drummoyne and oriented in a north-east/south-west direction. Cockatoo Island lies to the north-cast and Spectacle Island to the north-west.

The building platform is retained in part by rubble stone walls and in part by concrete sea walls. On the south-eastern side the original foreshore outcrops survive to form part of the side of the ship shaped island below the Mess Deck Starboard Classroom and between buildings 9 and 10, the J Payne Memorial Building and Main Deck Museum. The upper level of the island includes the Port Waist and Rifle Range, the Quarterdeck and the site of the Main Deck which define the original island areas. The lower level includes the Forecastle Deck, After End and the sites of buildings 3-6, 7-9 and 14. The Port Waist and part of the Quarterdeck are cement paved in common with areas between buildings 9-10. Capstan Flat comprises lawn and concrete pathways.

The island was planned to reflect the layout of ships at sea, the Main Deck building flanked by Port and Starboard Waists with Quarter Deck and After End at the stern and the Forecastle Deck at the prow. The Officers Accommodation is clearly separated from other ranks and closely associated with the Signal Station and Quarterdeck towards the stern. The Port and Starboard wharves (Items 1, 15), which provide access, are of timber construction as is the slatted boat Whaler ramp adjacent to building 6 (the Boatshed, Workshop and Store) and a number of walkways. The Baths and Tidal pools have collapsed. The main approach to the island on the north western side is identified by the two cabbage tree palms planted in the 1930s and the Officers accommodation. Buildings, which define the islands naval superstructure, and illustrate its cadet and museum roles, include the following.

• Guard House (Item 2);

• Officers Wardroom Mess (3);

• Generator Room (4);

• Mens and Womens Toilet Blocks (5, 7);

• Boat Shed, Workshop and Store (6);

• Gymnasium (8);

• J Payne Memorial Building (9);

• Main Deck-Museum (10);

• Mess Deck-Starboard Classroom (11);

• Signal Station (12);

• Officers Cottage (14); and

• Flammable Store (16).

These main buildings which define the ship-like planning and layout are predominantly corrugated galvanised iron and timber, with a combination of hipped and skillion roof forms. A signal mast adds to the maritime character.

Other features illustrating the training role of the island include boat davits, a capstan, derricks, bollards and winches and signal masts. Other site features include a range of functional items from shipping, a memorial to Captain Bligh, cannon, anchors, a steam engine, naval field guns, gun barrels from Hood, Repulse and Renown, and the 1932 foundation stone laid by Miss Charles Fairfax on 21 May 1932. The island also contains a number of individual items of note including the original Austral petrol driven electric generator of 1931.

A small area of the original rock outcrops of the island remain which may contain archaeological evidence of former uses.

**History**

Snapper Island, levelled and cleared in 1931, was originally a low sandstone island, with rocky shores and sparse vegetation. Recorded in 1788, on early maps of Sydney Harbour, Snapper Island was part of the 'Hen & Chickens' group in 1827. In April 1879 the island was dedicated as a public reserve, following a decision of the NSW Legislative Assembly, and in 1892 Trustees were appointed.

The first use of the island for other than occasional recreational visits was after 1900 when part of the island was used to store timber. In 1913 Snapper Island was included in the 'naval waters' surrounding Cockatoo Island dockyards, and in 1918, at the end of the First World War, was leased to the dockyard for use in storing ships parts and materials.

In 1921 Len Forsythe founded the first Navy League Sea Cadet companies at Drummoyne and North Sydney as companies within the NSW branch of the Navy League UK. The Drummoyne Depot grew to include some 70 persons by 1929, but was unable to continue to rent adequate premises. With the assistance of Jack Payne, Managing Director of Cockatoo Island Dockyard, Forsythe was able to rent Snapper Island for 15 pounds per year. However, in order to use the island effectively, it was found that the surface had to be levelled by blasting and excavation. In addition the area was increased by one third, with the construction of sea walls and fill, until its shape represented a ship. Buildings and sea baths were also constructed.

A Royal Navy Reserve had been founded in Britain in 1859 and in 1867 special cadetships were in place to train future officers. Formed without government support, the Sea Cadets movement can claim the longest continuous history of any British youth movement, dating back to before the Crimean War when sailors returning home from the campaign formed Naval Lads Brigades to help children in the sea port back streets. So successful were the Brigades in helping disadvantaged youth that the Navy League adopted them in 1910. The Navy League in Britain in 1910 included a national membership of a quarter of a million persons dedicated to supporting the Royal Navy. Training was along Naval and Merchant Marine Lines with ranks and ratings equivalent to the Navy. The fundamental principle of the Navy League in the 1930s was 'Complete protection for British subjects and British commerce the world over'.

HMAS Sydney, the first vessel constructed for the Royal Australian Navy in 1913, and the first RAN ship to sink an enemy warship, the Emden, had been an inspiration to Forsythe. When the Sydney was broken up in 1932 at the Sutherland Dock, on Cockatoo Island, Forsythe obtained substantial remnants including the binnacle, engine room telegraph, steering wheel and deck rails which now enclose the island's 'Main Deck', for 30 pounds. Many other items were removed from the Sydney and the compass from the Emden was added to the growing collection of naval and maritime heritage. Over the years this collection, the Len Forsythe Collection, was augmented from many sources; donations included the mast of HMAS Sydney.

In 1932 the island developed rapidly with the construction of new and relocated buildings. These included the former Drummoyne Depot buildings (Main Deck, Signal Building and Guard House). A wharf was completed and the complex linked by decks, bridges and gangways in simulation of life on board ship. Paired palm trees identified the approaches to the island. On 26 November 1932 the facility was officially opened by Sir Charles Cox, on behalf of the Minister for Defence, as a living memorial to HMAS Sydney. Snapper Island became the headquarters of Sydney's naval cadet groups. There are now some 70 units in Australia.

From the beginning Forsythe's objectives had been to 'teach boys from 10 years of age habits of discipline, duty and self-respect'. By September 1938 cadet numbers had risen to 110 with 75 on the waiting list. The island now included accommodation, a sick bay, ward-room, gunners store, general store and officers mess and a petrol driven electric generator. The unit's fleet included two whalers, one cutter, four motor boats and skiffs and dinghies. In November 1938 the J Payne Memorial Building was officially opened by Prime Minister Joe Lyons.

Following the onset of the Second World War the island was taken over by the RAN Naval Guard and from 1942-43 a gun crew was stationed on the island, followed by B Wing Water Transport of the Australian Imperial Forces and No2 Independent Troop, Great Britain Maritime Royal Artillery. During the period 1939-45 various new buildings were erected and further land reclaimed.

The island was handed back to the cadets on 22 January 1946, when a Women's Division was established in the Officers Cottage. By 1947 200 cadets were using the island, when the Navy League of Australia was formed to replace the former Navy League UK. In 1954 the island became 'Training Ship Sydney' as part of the newly formed Australian Sea Cadet Corps.

In 1952 the museum had been opened to the public and in 1960 the Len Forsythe Museum was opened in the Main Deck building coinciding with the formation of the Snapper Island Company as a registered charity. In 1976 the unit decided not to be absorbed into the Royal Naval Reserve Cadets, based at Spectacle Island, in order to retain its identity. Snapper Island continues to be leased to the Snapper Island Company Ltd, which maintains the island and continues to operate the fleet in conjunction with the local community and TAFE and school groups.

**Condition and Integrity**

**Integrity:**

The Preliminary Heritage Study of February 2001, by Clive Lucas Stapleton and Partners, indicates that the fabric of the place is essentially intact

**Condition:**

The general condition can be best described as run down due to lack of the ongoing maintenance usual for places in such marine environments.

The building structures appear to be in reasonable order however they have suffered serious termite problems in the past and active termite infestations are likely. (February 2001)

The DASCEM report of March 2000 has identified potential contaminants.

**Location**

About 1.65ha, in Sydney Harbour, 300 metres northeast of Birkenhead Point, between Balmain and Drummoyne.

**Bibliography**

• Clive Lucas, Stapleton & Partners Pty Ltd, Snapper Island Preliminary Heritage Study, for the Department of Finance and Administration February 2001.

• Navy League, NSW Branch, The Navy Journal, Sydney, 1938

• Garrett, Richard, The British Sailor, Wayland, London, 1974

• DASCEM Holdings Pty Ltd, Environmental Audit and Management Plan Snapper Island for Property Group, Report No. CL443-March 200

Appendix D ERM-Finance Significance Ranking Guide

**Significance Ranking For Commonwealth Heritage Listed Properties**

Ranking significance assists with identifying management priorities in the first instance. Ranking also assists with determining if a property meets the threshold for inclusion on the Commonwealth Heritage List (CHL). The criteria state that a place needs to have “significant” value in order to meet one or more criterion.

Ranking of significance is also a tool to be used in the development of management recommendations, maintenance priorities and long term planning decisions. Secondarily, they can be used in support of funding and resource allocations.

Therefore, ranking is a critical component underpinning specific management planning development.

The significance rankings described here are divided into three categories – Item, Precinct/Group and Intangible. These categories are based on our experience with large and complex sites and with managing European, Indigenous and Natural values. This allows a more meaningful use of ranking when:

Comparing of individual items and precincts within the site itself (ie multiple items which may have varying degrees of significance based on their context, integrity and condition);

Comparing a property with other similar sites (eg two buildings of comparable significance at 2 different sites may have settings of differing significance, thereby allowing a clearer comparison and more informed and secure basis for the overall ranking);

Identifying CH values across the site and making management recommendations specific to those defining qualities; and

Providing a context to the ranking where an element may be contributory rather than significant as an individual item. This underpins management of the item as a part of a larger context and assists in prioritising maintenance resources.

The values identified can then either be managed under the CH provisions or broader environmental requirements of the *Environment Protection and Biodiversity Conservation Act* (EPBC Act) depending on whether the CH threshold has been met. We find that this system allows us to be more specific about why a place has value, which in turn helps us to develop more targeted management methods.

The ranking system has been developed in reference to the ICOMOS, Burra Charter, World Heritage Guidelines, Ask First Guideline for Indigenous places and the Natural Heritage Charter. We have included “universal” level ranking for identifying potential national or World Heritage values.

The tables below outline the categories and ranking levels for built and Indigenous values\
(Table D-1) and natural values (Table D-2)

Table D-1 Summary of Significance Rankings for Built and Indigenous Heritage

| **Significance Ranking** | **Justification - Item** | **Justification – Precinct/Group** | **Justification - Intangible** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Universal (only to be used for World Heritage Sites) | Monuments: architectural works, works of monumental sculpture and painting, elements or structures of an archaeological nature, inscriptions, cave dwellings and combinations of features, which are of outstanding universal value from the point of view of history, art or science. | Groups of buildings: groups of separate or connected buildings which, because of their architecture, their homogeneity or their place in the landscape, are of outstanding universal value from the point of view of history, art or science. | Sites: works of man or the combined works of nature and man, and areas including archaeological sites which are of outstanding universal value from the historical, aesthetic, ethnological or anthropological point of view. |
| Exceptional | The item is a demonstrably rare, outstanding and / or an irreplaceable example of its type.<br>It has a high degree of intact and original fabric that is readily interpreted.<br>Loss or alteration would substantively undermine the Commonwealth heritage values of the place overall. | The precinct/group demonstrates collective characteristics that are rare or unique in Australia.<br>Precinct/group is intact and readily interpreted.<br>Loss, alteration or removal of component elements would substantially undermine the CH values of the place overall. | The site represents significant social, cultural, natural and/or mythological values that may not be embodied in any physical item but which demonstrate unique, iconic markers in Australia’s past or ongoing dynamic histories and / or processes. |
| High | The item demonstrates a rare example of its type.<br>Is largely intact and interpretable.<br>Loss or unsympathetic alteration may diminish the Commonwealth Heritage values of the item and of the place overall. | The precinct/group demonstrates a rare example of collective characteristics or features physically linking or defining the space.<br>Precinct/group is largely intact and interpretable.<br>Loss, unsympathetic alteration or removal of component elements or defining qualities may detract from the CH values of the precinct/group and of the site overall. | The site represents important social, cultural, natural and/or mythological values that may not be embodied in any physical item but which demonstrate rare points in Australia’s past or ongoing dynamic histories and / or processes |
| Moderate | The item may have altered or modified elements.<br>Item is intact enough to be partially interpretable as a single item or as part of the site in its entirety.<br>Loss or unsympathetic alteration is likely to diminish the CH values of the item and potentially the place if inappropriately managed. | Precinct/group demonstrates valuable (although modified) collective characteristics and linking/defining spatial qualities.<br>Precinct/group intact enough to be interpreted as a discrete space or as part of the site overall.<br>Loss, unsympathetic alteration or removal of component elements or defining qualities may detract from the CH values of the precinct/group and potentially of the site overall if inappropriately managed. | The site represents social, cultural, natural and/or mythological values that may not be embodied in any physical item but which demonstrate points in Australia’s past or ongoing dynamic histories and / or processes. |
| Low | The item may be largely altered.<br>Does not demonstrate the key defining qualities of the CH values, but may be contributory.<br>Alteration and / or modification may make it difficult to interpret the item depending on the existing integrity of the item.<br>Loss may not diminish the CH values of the place overall. | Precinct/group demonstrates some (but possibly largely altered) collective characteristics and/or linking/defining spatial qualities.<br>Precinct/group not easily interpreted and represents unclear spatial definition in relation to the rest of the site.<br>Loss, alteration or removal of component elements may not detract from the CH values of the place overall. | The site represents some social, cultural, natural and/or mythological values that may not be embodied in any physical item but which demonstrate points in the site’s history, associative values and/or historical themes. |
| None (Does not meet CHL criteria) | Item does not reflect or demonstrate any CH values. | Precinct/group does not reflect or demonstrate any CH values. | The site represents no social, cultural, natural or methodological themes or values. |
| Intrusive | Potentially detracts from the overall Commonwealth heritage values of the place as an intrusive element.<br>Loss may actually contribute to the Commonwealth Heritage values of the place.<br>The item is an intrusive element in the heritage values of the place in its entirety. | Precinct/group potentially detracts from the interpretation and understanding of the site overall.<br>Loss, alteration or removal of component elements actually contribute to the CH values of the place overall. | N/A |

Table D-2 Summary of Significance Ranking for Natural Heritage Values

| Significance Ranking | Justification - Natural |
|---|---|
| Universal (only to be used for World Heritage Sites) | **Natural features** consisting of physical and biological formations or groups of such formations, which are of outstanding universal value from the aesthetic or scientific point of view.<br>**Geological and physiographical formations** and precisely delineated areas which constitute the habitat of threatened species of animals and plants of outstanding universal value from the point of view of science or conservation.<br>**Natural sites** or precisely delineated natural areas of outstanding universal value from the point of view of science, conservation or natural beauty. |
| Exceptional | The species, area or ecosystem demonstrates individual or collective characteristics that are rare or unique in Australia.<br>Species, area or ecosystem is in high level of health, condition and integrity.<br>Loss, alteration or removal of component elements would substantially undermine the CH values of the place overall. |
| High | The species, area or ecosystem demonstrates a rare example of individual or collective characteristics or features physically linking or defining space.<br>Species, area or ecosystem is largely intact and in good state of health.<br>Loss, damage or removal of components or defining qualities may detract from the CH values of the area or ecosystem and of the site overall. |
| Moderate | Area or ecosystem demonstrates valuable (although modified) qualities.<br>Intact enough to be interpreted as a discrete space or as part of the site overall with ability to be regenerated.<br>Loss, damage or removal of component elements or defining qualities may detract from the CH values of the area or ecosystem and potentially of the site overall if inappropriately managed. |
| Low | Species, area or ecosystem demonstrates some (but possibly largely altered) defining qualities.<br>Area or ecosystem not in a good state of health and regeneration in doubt.<br>Loss, alteration or removal of component elements may not detract from the CH values of the place overall. |
| None (Does not meet CHL criteria) | Species, area or ecosystem does not reflect or demonstrate any CH values. |
| Intrusive | Loss, alteration or removal of component elements actually contribute to the CH values of the place overall. |

Appendix E Heritage Interpretation Strategy

## E.1 Heritage Interpretation Strategy

The guiding principles underpinning the development of this HIS are adopted from the Australia ICOMOS Charter for Places of Cultural Significance (the Burra Charter) (2013) which is considered the best practice standard for cultural heritage management in Australia. The Burra Charter defines interpretation as ‘all the ways of presenting the cultural significance of a place. Interpretation may be a combination of the treatment of fabric (e.g. maintenance, restoration, reconstruction); the use of and activities at the place; and the use of the introduced explanatory material.’

Interpretation is widely regarded as an ‘essential component of heritage conservation efforts and as a means of enhancing public appreciation and understanding of cultural heritage sites’, as defined by the ICOMOS Charter for the Interpretation and Presentation of Cultural Heritage Sites (Ename Charter) (2008).

This document presents a strategy for enhancing the interpretive capacity of Snapper Island and increasing awareness and understanding of the importance of the place and its heritage values. Snapper Island holds significant historical, associative, aesthetic and social heritage values; it remains a unique and important example of the work and contribution of the Navy League cadets to the operations of the Australian Navy. This interpretation strategy presents ideas for interpretive content, use of interpretive media, and a recommended process for implementation. The intention of this Interpretation Plan is to identify initial ideas and concepts for Finance to promote the significant heritage values of Snapper Island.

### E1.1 Methodology

Any potential interpretive strategies to be employed at Snapper Island must be developed in consultation with stakeholders. This strategy has been guided by the following best practice documents:

#### E1.1.1 International Charters

• The Burra Charter (2013) The Australia ICOMOS Charter for Places of Cultural Significance Australia ICOMOS (current version adopted 31.10.2013);

• ICOMOS Australia Practice Note: Interpretation, Version 1: November 2013; and

• Ename Charter: the charter for the interpretation and presentation of cultural heritage sites, 2008, ICOMOS.

#### E1.1.2 Australian Guidelines

• Interpreting Heritage Places and Items Guidelines, New South Wales Heritage Office (2005) Heritage Information Series: Endorsed by the Heritage Council of New South Wales;

• Australian Historic Themes Framework, a framework for use in heritage assessment and management. Department of Environment and Energy (2001) Australian Heritage Commission; and

• We’re a Dreaming Country: Guidelines for Interpretation of Aboriginal Heritage National Trust of Australia (WA) (2012).

### E1.2 Project Context

#### E.1.2.1 Situation Appraisal

There is currently no existing interpretation of the heritage values of Snapper Island. Potential interpretation opportunities exist in providing more easily accessible information about the island. These generally fall under two broad categories: person-to-person, which may include guided tours or information talks; or person-to-object, by using a range of interpretive media (online website, interpretive signage etc.) where the audience experiences the interpretation independently via different media or independent learning mechanisms.

The selection of appropriate media is determined through the background research, audience identification and an understanding of the site, its layout, functions and constraints. The interpretation of the heritage values of Snapper Island could comprise both on-site and off-site options; these interpretative opportunities as well as the constraints are discussed in detail below.

#### E1.2.2 Audience

Successful interpretation engages an audience and provokes their interest and reflection about the item or place and its values in a way that supports, sustains, and may even enhance its significance. Interpretation has proven to strengthen and sustain relationships between the community and its heritage and provide economic and social benefits (NSW Heritage Office, 2005a: 4-5).

Public access to the site is not currently permitted; external visitors and contractors attend the site. As such, the development of digital media as initial interpretive content would be preferable. This would allow a wide audience to understand the historic context and heritage values without having to visit the site.

There is also an opportunity to develop other forms of interpretation including interpretive signage, to benefit external visitors/contractors and also should public access be permitted to the site as part of the future use of Snapper Island.

#### E1.2.3 Key Interpretive Themes

The identification of key historic themes and storylines is important in providing a framework for the understanding of the cultural significance of a place or heritage item. It also assists in providing knowledge of places of historical importance that might not otherwise be identified, or for which there may be no tangible evidence. Identifying key historic themes and storylines associated with a heritage place assists in developing its appropriate interpretation and communicating its heritage values meaningfully.

An overview of the history of the Site is provided in Section 4.2. The Australian Historic Themes Framework has been used to identify the relevant historic themes for the Site. Table E-1 explores the Australian Historic Themes and NSW Historic Themes relevant to Snapper Island.

### E1.3 Interpretive Media

The choice of interpretation media at Snapper Island would be constrained by considerations of safety and security and should be hardy and easily repairable. The following sections provide guidance for the design of interpretive media, including materials and methods, siting options and design options.

#### E1.3.1 Person-to-object Opportunities

##### E1.3.1.1 Website and Digital Media

The development of interpretive content to feature on the website of Snapper Island’s owner should be considered. This would allow the general public to view historic information relating to the Island prior to the provision of public access.

A Quick Response (QR) code is a specific matrix barcode (or two-dimensional code), readable by dedicated QR barcode readers and phone cameras. The addition of QR codes to interpretive signs allows the immediate connection of interested viewers to further detailed information and digital content (including video and audio). QR codes are easy to generate, cost-effective and simple to access; as such, they are used widely in interpretive content.

##### E1.3.1.2 Interpretive Signage and Display Cases

Interpretive signage provides an appropriate and cost-effective option for communicating the history and significance of a heritage place as they can reach a wide audience and are low maintenance. Signage can take a variety of forms, ranging from a single image transferred onto a vertical surface, to a standalone sign with a combination of text, images, maps or other graphics.

Discrete interpretive panels or posters with historical photographs for reference could also be placed around Snapper Island and the buildings to enhance interpretation not just for external visitors but also for the future users/visitors of the island.

###### Materials and Methods

The siting and presentation of interpretive signs ensures the information is conveyed is appreciated by a wide audience. Interpretative signs should balance images and text such that the text is not dominant but adds meaning to the images. Appropriate acknowledgement of the source of images can invite further investigation of the historic photographs held by the local resources. Interpretive signage should be situated in areas that are freely accessible with frequent foot traffic; however, they should be strategically placed in order for people to comfortably stop and read.

Exterior, free-standing interpretive signage is appropriate for use at Snapper Island; signs could be erected outside of the extant buildings (and in locations of former buildings) to illustrate their historic uses.

In terms of their physical form, mid-height simple platform or lectern signs are considered suitable options. The fabric of the signs should respond to the layout, landscape and character of the site selected for interpretation; consideration of climate is also important to ensure longevity of materials. Suitable fabrics are timber or marine grade metals such as aluminium or steel. Aluminium or steel can be supplied powder coated or corten (a finish that rusts naturally to form a protective coating).

Interpretive information can be applied to the sign using a variety of methods. Digital prints can be used for full colour graphics and images, sealed with a protective layer to avoid damage. Screen printing on vinyl or direct to aluminium would also be appropriate. Sizing specifications of an A2 landscape orientation (42.0 cm x 59.4 cm) would be suitable.

Consideration should be made for the salvage of moveable heritage; timber beams or signs within Building 10 could be re-used and integrated into the interpretation. The timber could be utilised as framing for interpretive signage. Internal interpretive signage and displays could also be incorporated within buildings at Snapper Island. Building 10, formerly the Museum at the site, would be a suitable location. Consideration should be given to returning the Len Forsythe Collection to Snapper Island for use within the interpretation. Small moveable heritage items may be featured within display cabinets; the remaining display cabinets in Building 10 could be cleaned and re-used for some of these displays. Alternatively, a four-leg table top or boxed display case would be excellent for historic maps, as well as other small items. Another example is an upright integrated display board that includes both posters and moveable heritage.

##### E1.3.1.3 Interpretation Integrated with the Landscape

The content and locations for interpretive media should reflect the place they seek to explain. Interpretive signage may be designed to be experiential and subtle, to give a sense of a place and historic themes rather than an explicit retelling of the site’s history. This form of interpretation can be abstract and artistic. The approach for this form of interpretation could include ground inlays; ground inlays with appropriate words or images can stimulate interest and be used to mark the location of former structures. Integrating objects and text within a landscape can convey a story in a simple but meaningful way.

Consideration could be made for the interpretation of former buildings at Snapper Island (namely Buildings 11 or 12) through landscaping (plantings outlining the former footprints). Additionally, there are a number of coils of rope on the island; these could be used as a form of interpretation (Photograph E-1).

Photograph E-1 Coil of Rope at Snapper Island (ERM 2023)

[image: The photo of a coil of rope at Snapper Island placed on a concrete surface near water, partially covered by grass and shadows. Surrounding elements include a metal railing and a small sign, indicating an outdoor waterfront setting.]

### E1.4 Person-to-Person Opportunities

#### E1.4.1 Heritage Inductions

Heritage inductions could, and should, be offered to personnel working on Snapper Island including, contractors and be tailored for each. The inductions would include discussion of heritage values, site management compliance requirements, and a summary of historical land use.

#### E1.4.2 Guided Tours

There is an opportunity for guided heritage tours to be undertaken at Snapper Island, following establishment of a sustainable use of the facilities. These tours may operate in association with those at Cockatoo and Spectacle Islands. Consideration could be made for allocating specific “open days” or visiting hours where guided tours or information talks could be provided at Snapper Island.

<td><b>Early History of Snapper Island</b><br>Snapper Island is a small knoll, essentially a highpoint along the sandstone outcrops that make up Sydney Harbour, which were flooded around 6000 years ago. Snapper Island would have been an ideal vantage point for fishing; it is likely that Aboriginal communities spear fished there, and collected shellfish and molluscs.<br>In the early nineteenth century, Snapper Island was known as one of the ‘Hen & Chickens’, along with Cockatoo and Spectacle Islands. In 1879, Snapper Island was declared a public reserve and was made available as parkland.<br>Snapper Island was acquired by the Royal Australian Navy 1913 and was used as a temporary store for ship parts in connection with the Cockatoo Dock.</td>

<td>[image: The photo from archives of young cadets at snapper island with Main Deck at the background. C1930 (NAA: A1200, L36287)]<br>Photograph E-3 Young Cadets at Snapper Island, with Main Deck in background, c1930 (nAA: A1200, L36287)</td>
<td rowspan="3"><b>Naval Training for Cadets, Sydney Training Depot</b><br>In 1911 compulsory military (army) and naval training was introduced in Australia for all boys from the year that they turned 12.<br>By 1931, Snapper Island had been chosen as ideal for the training of naval cadets and the establishment of a naval depot lead by Leonard Forsythe (1894-1981). Forsythe was a returned Australian Infantry Forces (AIF) man, who established the Parramatta River Navy League Sea Cadet Company at Drummoyne and the Sydney Company at Iron Cove in the 1920s.<br>Between 1931 and 1932 works were undertaken by the small company of sea cadets (unaided by outside labour and financial assistance) to prepare Snapper Island for the establishment of the Sydney Training Depot (named after HMAS Sydney). The dense vegetation was cleared, and the surface of the island was lowered by nine feet to gain a flat surface for constructing buildings. A thousand tonnes of rock was blasted and the island was levelled and reclaimed; in total, the area of the island was increased by one-third. Forsythe had the vision to turn the small island into a ‘ship’. Sea walls were constructed around the island to form a ‘bow’ and ‘stern’. The Sydney Training Depot was officially opened on 26 November 1932.<br>A number of buildings were relocated from the naval depot at Drummoyne (including the Guard House, Main Deck and Signal Station). New buildings were also constructed at Snapper Island (including the Commanding Officer’s Cottage, Toilets and Boatshed), along with a miniature rifle range. The Jack Payne Memorial Building was constructed later in 1938.<br>Boys aged 10 to 14 would enter into the boys division; boys from 14 to 18 into the cadet division and from 18 upwards into the seamen division. The Sydney Training Depot was known for its thoroughness, cleanliness and discipline. Cadets learned a full range of maritime skills and all aspects of life on a naval boat. They also learned radio</td>

The background includes a large chart labeled "Fear God Honour the Queen," various flags, and mounted artifacts, highlighting historical military themes and ceremonial elements. C1930S (NAA: A1200, L36281)]<br>Photograph E-6 Main Deck, Snapper Island featuring relics from HMAS Sydney, c1930s (NAA: A1200, L36281)</td>
<td rowspan="2"><b>Commemoration of HMAS Sydney and Len Forsythe Collection</b><br>The commemoration of HMAS Sydney through the naming of Sydney Training Depot coincided with the scrapping of the war ship. Relics from the original HMAS Sydney were salvaged and displayed on the Main Deck at Snapper Island, including doors; engine room telegraphs; hand and steam steering wheels; and a binnacle. A perfect scale model of the ship was designed and made by the Commander and two cadets. These relics formed the nucleus of the museum collection later developed by Leonard Forsythe.<br>The Snapper Island Unit later became known as the Training Ship Sydney Unit, and by 1960, Forsythe formed Sydney Training Depot Snapper Island Ltd, so that the activities of the group would continue after his death. It was in the same year that Forsythe opened a museum of artefacts and curiosities from Australia’s naval history on Snapper Island. By 2000, there were no cadets being trained at Snapper Island and general public access had ceased unless by appointment. Up until 2006, Sydney Training Depot Snapper Island Ltd operated the museum on the island and looked after the Len Forsythe collection. The museum collection was subsequently removed to Spectacle Island and the Navy Museum at Garden Island and the island has remained unoccupied.<br>A small number of moveable heritage items remain at Snapper Island, with the majority housed within the Main Deck/Museum (Building 10) and the J Payne Memorial Building (Building 9). These include signs, boat stands, bed frames, cupboards/lockers, rope coils, metal equipment, domestic items, radio (club station) and other electrical equipment, and Snapper Island navy badges.</td>
</tr>

### E1.6 Conclusions and Recommendations

The aim of heritage interpretation is to communicate the significance of a heritage item or place through the identification of key themes and storylines that will convey a meaningful understanding of the history of a place, and its heritage values. This HIS has outlined key themes, stories and images as well as various interpretive media to inform the development of heritage interpretation for Snapper Island.

This strategy has identified that both person-to-object and person-to-person interpretive opportunities are present at Snapper Island.

The following are key recommendations that aim to guide the approach to heritage interpretation for Snapper Island:

RECOMMENDATION 1:

This HIS is a preliminary document, and should be issued to relevant stakeholders including the Sydney Harbour Federation Trust to provide input and feedback. Following consultation and approval from stakeholders, a Heritage Interpretation Plan (HIP) should be prepared, which essentially moves the HIS from concept to detailed planning following concept determination/approval. The HIP would outline policies, roles and responsibilities for future implementation of the interpretation strategy, and a plan for timing and scope of installation, and routine maintenance guidance for each interpretive device.

RECOMMENDATION 2:

Consideration should be made for both person-to-object, and person-to-person heritage interpretation at Snapper Island. The erection of interpretive signage, development of website, and heritage inductions and guided tours are considered viable options.

RECOMMENDATION 3:

Consideration should be given to returning the Len Forsythe Collection to Snapper Island for use within heritage interpretation, following the rehabilitation of buildings in accordance with the HMP and determination of future of the site. Consideration could also be made to the salvage of moveable heritage within Buildings 9 and 10 for incorporation within heritage interpretation. For example, timber framework could be re-used as framing for interpretive signage, and small moveable heritage items may be featured within display cabinets.

### E1.7 Further Information

A series of guidelines about the interpretation and presentation of Heritage Places is accessible through the ICOMOS Australia website:

http://australia.icomos.org/resources/australia-icomos-heritage-toolkit/interpretation-presentation-of-heritage-places/

The ICOMOS International Scientific Committee on Interpretation and Presentation of Cultural Heritage Sites (ICIP) arm of the ICOMOS International has been developed specifically to study the evolving technologies and techniques of public interpretation and presentation. Information about developing interpretive methodologies, conservation, emerging interpretive technologies and industry best practice standards can be found on their ICIP website: http://icip.icomos.org/

Appendix F Physical Analysis

## F1 Physical Analysis

### F1.1 Building 2 – Guard House

The Guard House is situated on the far port side of the island, at the end of the port wharf. The building acts at the entry way to Snapper Island and is labelled with the name in bold letters. The structure is timber and corrugated iron, with a narrow breezeway running through the building from the jetty to the east. There is a bench on the southern side of the breezeway. A small room to the north of the hallway contains pieces of furniture including a desk and chair. The structure is in fair condition, with some corrosion. There appears to be no major structural damage to the building (Photograph F-1 to Photograph F-5).

### F1.2 Building 3 – Officer’s Wardroom Mess

The Officer’s Wardroom Mess is a small single storey timber and corrugated iron structure with brick foundations. It is located at the aft end of the island and is connected to the Officer’s Cottage (Building 14) via a roofed concrete breezeway (Photograph F-6 and Photograph F-7). The interior of the building contains timber flooring. At the western end, seating is attached to the southern, western, and northern walls. A serving bench, shelving and a sink exist at the eastern end of the building. The interior walls contain a large amount of mould (Photograph F-8 and Photograph F-9). The building is in in fair condition, with corrosion and flaking paint noted (as is common across the island).

### F1.4 Building 6 – Boatshed and Workshop

The Boatshed and Workshop are located on the port side of the island, to the north of the guardhouse. The Boatshed/workshop are timber framed structures with corrugated iron sheeting on the exterior. The Boatshed is open at the northern end, the workshop has a small opening at the western end. The structures are in very poor condition, with hazard signage indicating asbestos contamination and unstable surfaces. The open end of the Boatshed shows the poor condition of the flooring and of the internal framing – some of which has decayed and fallen. Leading from the Boatshed is a ramp constructed of timber and iron ‘I’ beams. The ‘I’ beams are heavily corroded and the timbers are water damaged (Photograph F-15 to Photograph F-16).

### F1.5 Building 8 – Gymnasium

The Gymnasium is a single storey timber and corrugated iron structure located just north of the J. Payne Memorial Building at the fore end of the island. Timber and corrugated iron is currently being stored in between the two buildings. The interior could not be accessed, as the door (at the starboard end) was locked and there was no available key. The structure is in poor to fair condition, with significant corrosion of the corrugated iron noted during the inspection (Photograph F-19 to Photograph F-23).

### F1.6 Building 9 – J. Payne Memorial Building

The J. Payne Memorial building is a two-storey timber frame corrugated iron structure situated on the lower deck at the fore end of the island. The structure contains bunks and storage rooms on the lower floor and a former recreation room on the upper floor. The upper floor has a double door on the aft side, which formerly lead to a walkway, which accessed the upper deck. This walkway has since collapsed and been removed and the door is no longer operable (Photograph F-24 and Photograph F-25).

The building has timber floors throughout and the timbers of the upper floor is still in good condition. The stairs have been removed and replaced with an aluminium ladder, allowing access to the upper floor. The walls are clad with plasterboard and timber framing, with timber doors and window frames throughout.

The lower floor has two sets of double doors leading to a covered walkway between the J. Payne building and the Gymnasium, on the aft side. The doors lead into a main bunk room, containing a number of steel frame bunk beds. There are a series of smaller rooms leading off the main bunk, including an officer’s quarters with lockers and additional beds; a storage/locker room; a small sick bay containing an upturned bed, in which the flooring has collapsed (not accessible during time of inspection); and a sitting room (Photograph F-26 and Photograph F-27).

The upper floor has a similar layout, featuring a central room with four smaller rooms branching off. The main room contains several chairs/couches, a billiards table with maps laid out across the top, and miscellaneous items including a typewriter and a ‘Hoover’ vacuum cleaner. The moveable heritage items located within the building are detailed in Appendix H. The four smaller rooms are located at the starboard and port ends. At the starboard end, one contains storage cupboards, and some speakers and a lamp affixed to the wall. The other room consists of a small kitchenette with cupboards and a fridge remaining. The items within the building are generally in poor condition; however, the structure itself is in a fair condition. At the port end, the rooms contain additional beds and storage units (Photograph F-28 and Photograph F-29).

### F1.7 Building 10 – Main Deck

The Main Deck is the most prominent building on Snapper Island, as it is centrally located and occupies the majority of the upper deck. The structure is single storey timber frame with corrugated iron cladding to the exterior. The roof is hipped with skillion surrounds; all clad with corrugated iron sheeting. The main entrance is through a set of double timber doors on the port side of the building, leading into the main hall of the deck. There are secondary entrances on the starboard and fore sides of the building, leading to the kitchen. There are also two fire exit doors on the fore and aft sides, although these have been permanently fixed closed since the abandonment of the building.

The interior of the structure is simple in design. There are 15 rooms branching off the main hall, some with remnant bulkhead fittings at the base of the entry. These were designed to simulate stepping over the partitions on a naval ship. The walls are clad with vertical timber boards which have been painted in pale blue. The roof framing and cladding is exposed with no remaining ceiling. The floors are timber boards, with some accent paintings in hallways and surrounding doors (Photograph F-31).

The aft end of the interior retains the paintings used to decorate the HMAS Sydney display area (Photograph F-32). No fittings remain in this location. The remainder of the main hall contains miscellaneous items, including several lockers, bench seats, folding tables (that would have been previously mounted to the walls), and domestic items (Photograph F-33). The moveable heritage items located within the building are detailed in Appendix H. The rooms branching off the main hall contain various other materials and objects, which have been left, including painting, footlockers, radio equipment, kitchen appliances and tools (Photograph F-34). There are some remaining display cases, demonstrating the former museum usage.

The structure is in fair condition, although there is significant dust, grime and bird droppings throughout the interior. No significant damage impacting the main structure was noted during the inspection. There is evidence of some water damage and peeling paint throughout, along with some carpets inside rooms which are in need of replacement (Photograph F-35). The concrete flooring in the kitchen has significant cracking and is in need of repair.

### F1.8 Building 14 – Officer’s Cottage

The Officer’s Cottage is a small single storey corrugated iron structure, probably with a timber frame (although this is not visible). The roof is a corrugated iron skillion, which was ‘peeling’ away from the building framing and severely damaged during the most recent inspection (Photograph F-36). The Cottage consists of two rooms, a living/dining/kitchen space and a bedroom, with an ensuite bathroom.

Access to the cottage is through a timber door into the living area, which contains a small kitchenette, a hearth/fireplace, and a sitting area. Timber floorboards are present throughout, except in the bathroom where carpet has been retained. The interior walls are timber cladding and plasterboard. The splashback to the kitchenette is linoleum or vinyl. The bedroom is empty, while the bathroom contains a toilet, small sink and medicine cabinet (Photograph F-37 to Photograph F-39).
