<td>Calidris melanotos</td>
<td>Pectoral Sandpiper</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>The Pectoral Sandpiper prefers shallow fresh to saline wetlands. The species is found at coastal lagoons, estuaries, bays, swamps, lakes, inundated grasslands, saltmarshes, river pools, creeks, floodplains and artificial wetlands.</td>
<td>Unlikely – suitable habitat absent from site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Calidris ruficollis</td>
<td>Red-necked Stint</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>The Red-necked Stint is mostly found in coastal areas, including in sheltered inlets, bays, lagoons and estuaries with intertidal mudflats, often near spits, islets and banks and, sometimes, on protected sandy or coralline shores. Occasionally they have been recorded on exposed or ocean beaches, and sometimes on stony or rocky shores, reefs or shoals.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely – suitable habitat absent from site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Charadrius bicinctus</td>
<td>Double-banded Plover</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>The Double-banded Plover is found on coastal beaches, mudflats, sewage farms, river banks, fields, dunes, upland tussock grasses and shingle.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Gallinago hardwickii</td>
<td>Latham's Snipe</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Latham's Snipe occurs in permanent and ephemeral wetlands up to 2000 m above sea-level. They usually inhabit open, freshwater wetlands with low, dense vegetation (e.g. swamps, flooded grasslands or heathlands, around bogs and other water bodies).</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Gallinago megala</td>
<td>Swinhoe's Snipe</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>During the non-breeding season Swinhoe's Snipe occurs at the edges of wetlands, such as wet paddy fields, swamps and freshwater streams. The species is also known to occur in grasslands, drier cultivated areas (including crops of rapeseed and wheat) and market gardens. Habitat specific to Australia includes the dense clumps of grass and rushes round the edges of fresh and brackish wetlands. This includes swamps, billabongs, river pools, small streams and sewage ponds. They are also found in drying claypans and inundated plains pitted with crab holes.</td>
<td>Unlikely – outside species known distribution.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Gallinago stenura</td>
<td>Pin-tailed Snipe</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>During non-breeding period the Pin-tailed Snipe occurs most often in or at the edges of shallow freshwater swamps, ponds and lakes with emergent, sparse to dense cover of grass/sedge or other vegetation. The species is also found in drier, more open wetlands such as claypans in more arid parts of species' range. It is also commonly seen at sewage ponds; not normally in saline or inter-tidal wetlands.</td>
<td>Unlikely – outside species known distribution.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Limosa limosa</td>
<td>Black-tailed Godwit</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>The Black-tailed Godwit is commonly found in sheltered bays, estuaries and lagoons with large intertidal mudflats or sandflats, or spits and banks of mud, sand or shell-grit; occasionally recorded on rocky coasts or coral islets. It is also found in shallow and sparsely vegetated, near-coastal, wetlands; such as saltmarsh, saltflats, river pools, swamps, lagoons and floodplains. There are a few inland records, around shallow, freshwater and saline lakes, swamps, dams and bore-overflows. They also use lagoons in sewage farms and saltworks.</td>
<td>Unlikely – outside species known distribution.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Numenius minutus</td>
<td>Little Curlew, Little Whimbrel</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>The Little Curlew is most often found feeding in short, dry grassland and sedgeland, including dry floodplains and blacksoil plains, which have scattered, shallow freshwater pools or areas seasonally inundated. Open woodlands with a grassy or burnt understorey, dry saltmarshes, coastal swamps, mudflats or sandflats of estuaries or beaches on sheltered coasts, mown lawns, gardens, recreational areas, ovals, racecourses and verges of roads and airstrips are also used.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent within site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Numenius phaeopus</td>
<td>Whimbrel</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>The Whimbrel is often found on the intertidal mudflats of sheltered coasts.</td>
<td>Unlikely - suitable habitat absent within site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pandion haliaetus</td>
<td>Osprey</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Eastern Ospreys occur in littoral and coastal habitats and terrestrial wetlands of tropical and temperate Australia and offshore islands. They are mostly found in coastal areas but occasionally travel inland along major rivers, particularly in northern Australia They require extensive areas of open fresh, brackish or saline water for foraging.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Philomachus pugnax</td>
<td>Ruff</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>In Australia the Ruff is found on generally fresh, brackish of saline wetlands with exposed mudflats at the edges. It is found in terrestrial wetlands including lakes, swamps, pools, lagoons, tidal rivers, swampy fields and floodlands.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent within site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pluvialis fulva</td>
<td>Pacific Golden Plover</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Pacific Golden Plovers usually occur on beaches, mudflats and sandflats (sometimes in vegetation such as mangroves, low saltmarsh such as Sarcocornia, or beds of seagrass) in sheltered areas including harbours, estuaries and lagoons, and also in evaporation ponds in saltworks.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years..</td>
<td>Unlikely – suitable habitat absent from site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Tringa brevipes</td>
<td>Grey-tailed Tattler</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>The Grey-tailed Tattler is often found on sheltered coasts with reefs and rock platforms or with intertidal mudflats. It can also be found at intertidal rocky, coral or stony reefs as well as platforms and islets that are exposed at low tide. This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely – suitable habitat absent from site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Tringa nebularia</td>
<td>Common Greenshank</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>The Common Greenshank is found in a wide variety of inland wetlands and sheltered coastal habitats of varying salinity. It occurs in sheltered coastal habitats, typically with large mudflats and saltmarsh, mangroves or seagrass. Habitats include embayments, harbours, river estuaries, deltas and lagoons and are recorded less often in round tidal pools, rock-flats and rock platforms.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent within site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Tringa stagnatilis</td>
<td>Marsh Sandpiper</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>The Marsh Sandpiper lives in permanent or ephemeral wetlands of varying salinity, including swamps, lagoons, billabongs, saltpans, saltmarshes, estuaries, pools on inundated floodplains, and intertidal mudflats and also regularly at sewage farms and saltworks.</td>
<td>Unlikely – suitable habitat absent from site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Phaethon lepturus</td>
<td>White-tailed Tropicbird</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Tropicbirds are predominantly pelagic species, rarely coming to shore except to breed. The white-tailed tropicbird forages in warm waters and over long distances, moving up to 1500 kilometres from breeding sites. It feeds on fish and cephalopods by plunge-diving. This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely – suitable habitat absent from site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Hirundapus caudacutus</td>
<td>White-throated Needletail</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>In Australia, the White-throated Needletail is almost exclusively aerial, from heights of less than 1 m up to more than 1000 m above the ground. Because they are aerial, it has been stated that conventional habitat descriptions are inapplicable, but there are, nevertheless, certain preferences exhibited by the species. Although they occur over most types of habitat, they are probably recorded most often above wooded areas, including open forest and rainforest, and may also fly between trees or in clearings, below the canopy, but they are less commonly recorded flying above woodland. They also commonly occur over heathland, but less often over treeless areas, such as grassland or swamps.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely – suitable habitat absent from site (aerial species).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Ardenna pacificus</td>
<td>Wedge-tailed Shearwater</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>The Wedge-tailed Shearwater is a pelagic, marine bird known from tropical and subtropical waters. The species tolerates a range of surface-temperatures and salinities, but is most abundant where temperatures are greater than 21 °C and salinity is greater than 34.6 %. In tropical zones the species may feed over cool nutrient-rich waters. The species has been recorded in offshore waters of eastern Victoria and southern NSW, mostly over continental slope with sea-surface temperatures of 13.9–24.4 °C and usually off the continental shelf in north-west Australia.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely – suitable habitat absent within site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Ardea ibis</td>
<td>Cattle Egret</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>The Cattle Egret occurs in tropical and temperate grasslands, wooded lands and terrestrial wetlands. The Cattle Egret often forages away from water on low lying grasslands, improved pastures and croplands.<br>This species has been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely - suitable habitat absent within site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Egretta sacra</td>
<td>Eastern Reef Egret</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>The Eastern Reef Egret lives on beaches, rocky shores, tidal rivers and inlets, mangroves, and exposed coral reefs. This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent within site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Erythrotriorchis radiatus</td>
<td>Red Goshawk</td>
<td>CE</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Red Goshawks inhabit open woodland and forest, preferring a mosaic of vegetation types, a large population of birds as a source of food, and permanent water, and are often found in riparian habitats along or near watercourses or wetlands. In NSW, preferred habitats include mixed subtropical rainforest, Melaleuca swamp forest and riparian Eucalyptus forest of coastal rivers.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely - suitable habitat absent within site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Stercorarius pomarinus</td>
<td>Pomarine Jaeger</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>During Migration, wanders tropical and subtropical seas. Occurs in Australian coastal water between October and May. Most common along the edge of continental shelf, scarce closer to shore and only rarely seen in harbours and estuaries.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely - suitable habitat absent within site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Hydroprogne caspia</td>
<td>Caspian Tern</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>The Caspian Tern is mostly found in sheltered coastal embayments (harbours, lagoons, inlets, bays, estuaries and river deltas) and those with sandy or muddy margins are preferred.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Potential - The species may ‘fly-over’ the site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Sterna hirundo</td>
<td>Common Tern</td>
<td></td>
<td>Mi</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Common Terns are marine, pelagic and coastal. In Australia, they are recorded in all marine zones, but are commonly observed in near-coastal waters, both on ocean beaches, platforms and headlands and in sheltered waters, such as bays, harbours and estuaries with muddy, sandy or rocky shores. Occasionally they are recorded in coastal and near-coastal wetlands, either saline or freshwater, including lagoons, rivers, lakes, swamps and saltworks. Sometimes they occur in mangroves or saltmarsh and, in bad weather, in coastal sand-dunes or coastal embayments.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Potential - The species may ‘fly-over’ the site,</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Glossopsitta pusilla</td>
<td>Little Lorikeet</td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Forages primarily in the canopy of open Eucalyptus forest and woodland, yet also finds food in Angophora, Melaleuca and other tree species. Riparian habitats are particularly used, due to higher soil fertility and hence greater productivity. Roosts in treetops, often distant from feeding areas.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Lathamus discolor</td>
<td>Swift Parrot</td>
<td>E</td>
<td>CE</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>On the mainland they occur in areas where eucalypts are flowering profusely or where there are abundant lerp (from sap-sucking bugs) infestations.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Fregetta grallaria grallaria</td>
<td>White-bellied Storm-Petrel (Tasman Sea), Whitebellied Storm-Petrel (Australasian)</td>
<td></td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>The White-bellied Storm-Petrel occurs across sub-tropical and tropical waters in the Tasman Sea, Coral Sea and, possibly, the central Pacific Ocean. In the non-breeding season, it reaches and forages over near-shore waters along the continental shelf of mainland Australia It breeds, in Australian territory, on offshore islets and rocks in the Lord Howe Island group.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Neophema chrysogaster</td>
<td>Orange-bellied Parrot</td>
<td>CE</td>
<td>CE</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Orange-bellied Parrots are seen almost exclusively in coastal and sub-coastal areas, preferring peninsulas and islands. Saltmarshes, littoral (shore) heathlands and low scrublands are preferred habitats as well as grassy areas, which can include golf courses.</td>
<td>Unlikely – suitable habitat absent from site area.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pterodroma leucoptera leucoptera</td>
<td>Gould's Petrel, Australian Gould's Petrel</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>E</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Breeds on both Cabbage Tree Island, 1.4 km offshore from Port Stephens and on nearby Boondelbah island. The range and feeding areas of non-breeding petrels are unknown.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pterodroma neglecta neglecta</td>
<td>Kermadec Petrel (western)</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Marine species. Breeds on islands across the South Pacific. In Australia it breeds on Ball's Pyramid and Phillip Island (near Norfolk Island). Vagrant birds occur in coastal NSW waters, particularly after storm events.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Frogs</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Heleioporus australiacus</td>
<td>Giant Burrowing Frog</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Found in heath, woodland and open dry sclerophyll forest on a variety of soil types except those that are clay based. Breeding habitat of this species is generally soaks or pools within first or second order streams</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent within site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Litoria aurea</td>
<td>Green and Golden Bell Frog</td>
<td>E</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Inhabits marshes, dams and stream-sides, particularly those containing bullrushes (Typha spp.) or spikerushes (Eleocharis spp.).<br>Optimum habitat includes water-bodies that are unshaded, free of predatory fish such as Plague Minnow (Gambusia holbrooki), have a grassy area nearby and diurnal sheltering sites available.<br>Some sites, particularly in the Greater Sydney region occur in highly disturbed areas.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent within site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pseudophryne australis</td>
<td>Red-crowned Toadlet</td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Occurs in open forests, mostly on Hawkesbury and Narrabeen Sandstones.<br>Inhabits periodically wet drainage lines below sandstone ridges that often have shale lenses or cappings.<br>Shelters under rocks and amongst masses of dense vegetation or thick piles of leaf litter.<br>Breeding congregations occur in dense vegetation and debris beside ephemeral creeks and gutters.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent within site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Reptiles</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Hoplocephalus bungaroides</td>
<td>Broad-headed Snake</td>
<td></td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Shelters in rock crevices and under flat sandstone rocks on exposed cliff edges during autumn, winter and spring.<br>Moves from the sandstone rocks to shelters in crevieces or hollows in large trees within 500m of escarpments in summer.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent within site</td>
</tr>

</tr>

<td>Chalinolobus dwyeri</td>
<td>Large-eared Pied Bat, Large Pied Bat</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Roosts in caves (near their entrances), crevices in cliffs, old mine workings and in the disused, bottle-shaped mud nests of the Fairy Martin (Petrochelidon ariel), frequenting low to mid-elevation dry open forest and woodland close to these features. Found in well-timbered areas containing gullies.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent within site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Dasyurus maculatus maculatus</td>
<td>Spot-tailed Quoll, Spotted-tail Quoll, Tiger Quoll (south-eastern mainland population)</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>E</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Recorded across a range of habitat types, including rainforest, open forest, woodland, coastal heath and inland riparian forest, from the sub-alpine zone to the coastline.Individual animals use hollow-bearing trees, fallen logs, small caves, rock outcrops and rocky-cliff faces as den sites.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent within site.</td>
</tr>

<td>Isoodon obesulus obesulus</td>

<td></td>
<td>E</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>They are generally only found in heath or open forest with a heathy understorey on sandy or friable soils.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent within site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Petrogale penicillata</td>
<td>Brush-tailed Rock-wallaby</td>
<td></td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Occupy rocky escarpments, outcrops and cliffs with a preference for complex structures with fissures, caves and ledges, often facing north.</td>
<td>Unlikely – site outside of this species known distribution</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Phascolarctos cinereus</td>
<td>Koala</td>
<td></td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Inhabit eucalypt woodlands and forests.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent within site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pseudomys novaehollandiae</td>
<td>New Holland Mouse, Pookila</td>
<td></td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>The New Holland Mouse has been found from coastal areas and up to 100 km inland on sandstone country The species has been recorded from sea level up to around 900 m above sea level.</td>
<td>Unlikely – site outside of this species known distribution</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pteropus poliocephalus</td>
<td>Grey-headed Flying-fox</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Occur in subtropical and temperate rainforests, tall sclerophyll forests and woodlands, heaths and swamps as well as urban gardens and cultivated fruit crops.<br>This species has been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Potential –foraging habitat present within site</td>
</tr>

<td>Long-nosed Bandicoot population in inner western Sydney</td>
<td>E</td>
<td></td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Shelter mostly under older houses and buildings. Forage in parkland and back-yards.<br>This species has been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent within site</td>
</tr>

<td>Eastern Pygmy-possum</td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Found in a broad range of habitats from rainforest through sclerophyll (including Box-Ironbark) forest and woodland to heath.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent within site</td>
</tr>

<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Roosts singly or in groups of up to six, in tree hollows and buildings; in treeless areas they are known to utilise mammal burrows. Forages in most habitats across its very wide range, with and without trees; appears to defend an aerial territory.<br>This species has been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>

<td>Eastern Freetail-bat</td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Occur in dry sclerophyll forest, woodland, swamp forests and mangrove forests east of the Great Dividing Range.<br>Roost mainly in tree hollows but will also roost under bark or in man-made structures.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>

<tr>

<td>Little Bentwing-bat</td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Moist eucalypt forest, rainforest, vine thicket, wet and dry sclerophyll forest, Melaleuca swamps, dense coastal forests and banksia scrub. Generally found in well-timbered areas. Little Bentwing-bats roost in caves, tunnels, tree hollows, abandoned mines, stormwater drains, culverts, bridges and sometimes buildings during the day, and at night forage for small insects beneath the canopy of densely vegetated habitats.<br>This species has been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Potential – potential roosting habitat present within site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Miniopterus schreibersii oceanensis</td>
<td>Eastern Bentwing-bat</td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Caves are the primary roosting habitat, but also use derelict mines, storm-water tunnels, buildings and other man-made structures. Hunt in forested areas.<br>This species has been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Potential – potential roosting habitat present within site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Myotis macropus</td>
<td>Southern Myotis</td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Generally roost in groups of 10 - 15 close to water in caves, mine shafts, hollow-bearing trees, storm water channels, buildings, under bridges and in dense foliage. Forage over streams and pools catching insects and small fish by raking their feet across the water surface.<br>This species has been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Potential – potential roosting habitat present within site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Insects</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Petalura gigantea</td>
<td>Giant Dragonfly</td>
<td>E</td>
<td></td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Live in permanent swamps and bogs with some free water and open vegetation. Adults spend most of their time settled on low vegetation on or adjacent to the swamp.<br>This species has been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent within site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Plants</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Acacia bynoeana</td>
<td>Bynoe’s Wattle, Tiny Wattle</td>
<td>E</td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Occurs in heath or dry sclerophyll forest on sandy soils. Seems to prefer open, sometimes slightly disturbed sites such as trail margins, edges of roadside spoil mounds and in recently burnt patches. Associated overstorey species include Red Bloodwood, Scribbly Gum, Parramatta Red Gum, Saw Banksia and Narrow-leaved Apple.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent within site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Acacia pubescens</td>
<td>Downy Wattle, Hairy Stemmed Wattle</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Occurs on alluviums, shales and at the intergrade between shales and sandstones. The soils are characteristically gravely soils, often with ironstone. Occurs in open woodland and forest, in a variety of plant communities, including Cooks River/Castlereagh Ironbark Forest, Shale/Gravel Transition Forest and Cumberland Plain Woodland.</td>
<td>Unlikely – commonly associated soil type does not occur on site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Acacia terminalis subsp. terminalis MS</td>
<td>Sunshine Wattle</td>
<td>E</td>
<td>E</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Coastal scrub and dry sclerophyll woodland on sandy soils. Habitat is generally sparse and scattered. Most areas of habitat or potential habitat are small and isolated. Most sites are highly modified or disturbed due to surrounding urban development.<br>This species has been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Caladenia tessellata</td>
<td>Thick-lipped Spider-orchid, Daddy Long-legs</td>
<td>E</td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Generally found in grassy sclerophyll woodland on clay loam or sandy soils, though the population near Braidwood is in low woodland with stony soil.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Cryptostylis hunteriana</td>
<td>Leafless Tongue-orchid</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Does not appear to have well defined habitat preferences and is known from a range of communities, including swamp-heath and woodland. The larger populations typically occur in woodland dominated by Scribbly Gum (Eucalyptus sclerophylla), Silvertop Ash (E. sieberi), Red Bloodwood (Corymbia gummifera) and Black Sheoak (Allocasuarina littoralis); appears to prefer open areas in the understorey of this community and is often found in association with the Large Tongue Orchid (C. subulata) and the Tartan Tongue Orchid (C. erecta).</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Genoplesium baueri</td>
<td>Yellow Gnat-orchid, Bauer’s Midge Orchid</td>
<td>E</td>
<td>E</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Grows in dry sclerophyll forest and moss gardens over sandstone.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Melaleuca biconvexa</td>
<td>Biconvex Paperbark</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Biconvex Paperbark generally grows in damp places, often near streams or low-lying areas on alluvial soils of low slopes or sheltered aspects.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Melaleuca deanei</td>
<td>Deane's Paperbark</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>The species occurs mostly in ridgetop woodland, with only 5% of sites in heath on sandstone.<br>This species has been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pelargonium sp. Striatellum</td>
<td>Omeo Stork’s-bill</td>
<td>E</td>
<td>E</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>It has a narrow habitat that is usually just above the high-water level of irregularly inundated or ephemeral lakes, in the transition zone between surrounding grasslands or pasture and the wetland or aquatic communities. It sometimes colonises exposed lake beds during dry periods.</td>
<td>Unlikely – site outside of this species known distribution</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Persoonia hirsuta</td>
<td>Hairy Geebung, Hairy Persoonia</td>
<td>E</td>
<td>E</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>The Hairy Geebung is found in sandy soils in dry sclerophyll open forest, woodland and heath on sandstone.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pimelea curviflora var. curviflora</td>
<td></td>
<td>V</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Occurs on shaley/lateritic soils over sandstone and shale/sandstone transition soils on ridgetops and upper slopes amongst woodlands.<br>This species has been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely – commonly associated soil type does not occur on site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Syzygium paniculatum</td>
<td>Magenta Lilly Pilly, Magenta Cherry, Daguba, Scrub Cherry, Creek Lilly Pilly, Brush Cherry</td>
<td>E</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>On the south coast the Magenta Lilly Pilly occurs on grey soils over sandstone, restricted mainly to remnant stands of littoral (coastal) rainforest.<br>This species has been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Thesium australe</td>
<td>Austral Toadflax, Toadflax</td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Occurs in grassland on coastal headlands or grassland and grassy woodland away from the coast. Often found in association with Kangaroo Grass (Themeda australis).</td>
<td>Unlikely – site outside of this species known distribution</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Tetratheca glandulosa</td>
<td></td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Associated with shale-sandstone transition habitat where shale-cappings occur over sandstone, with associated soil landscapes such as Lucas Heights, Gymea, Lambert and Faulconbridge. Topographically, the plant occupies ridgetops, upper-slopes and to a lesser extent mid-slope sandstone benches. Soils are generally shallow, consisting of a yellow, clayey/sandy loam. Stony lateritic fragments are also common in the soil profile on many of these ridgetops.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely – commonly associated geology absent from site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Tetratheca juncea</td>
<td>Black-eyed Susan</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>It is usually found in low open forest/woodland with a mixed shrub understorey and grassy groundcover. However, it has also been recorded in heathland and moist forest.<br>The majority of populations occur on low nutrient soils associated with the Awaba Soil Landscape.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely – site outside of this species known distribution</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Epacris purpurascens var. purpurascens</td>
<td></td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Found in a range of habitat types, most of which have a strong shale soil influence.<br>This species has been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely – commonly associated soil type does not occur on site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Prostanthera marifolia</td>
<td>Seaforth Mintbush</td>
<td>CE</td>
<td>CE</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Occurs in localised patches in or in close proximity to the endangered Duffys Forest ecological community. Located on deeply weathered clay-loam soils associated with ironstone and scattered shale lenses, a soil type which only occurs on ridge tops and has been extensively urbanised.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years (OEH 2018)</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Callistemon linearifolius</td>
<td>Netted Bottle Brush</td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Recorded from the Georges River to Hawkesbury River in the Sydney area, and north to the Nelson Bay area of NSW. Grows in dry sclerophyll forest on the coast and adjacent ranges.<br>This species has been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Eucalyptus nicholii</td>
<td>Narrow-leaved Black Peppermint</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Typically grows in dry grassy woodland, on shallow soils of slopes and ridges. Found primarily on infertile soils derived from granite or metasedimentary rock.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely – site outside of this species known distribution</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Eucalyptus pulverulenta</td>
<td>Silver-leafed Gum</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Grows in shallow soils as an understorey plant in open forest, typically dominated by Brittle Gum (Eucalyptus mannifera), Red Stringybark (E. macrorhynca), Broad-leafed Peppermint (E. dives), Silvertop Ash (E. sieberi) and Apple Box (E. bridgesiana) (OEH 2018).<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely – site outside of this species known distribution</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Leptospermum deanei</td>
<td></td>
<td>V</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Woodland on lower hill slopes or near creeks. Sandy alluvial soil or sand over sandstone. Occurs in Riparian Scrub - e.g. Tristaniopsis laurina, Baechea myrtifolia; Woodland - e.g. Eucalyptus haemstoma; and Open Forest - e.g. Angophora costata, Leptospermum trinervium, Banksia ericifolia.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site</td>

<tr>
<td>Hibbertia puberula</td>
<td></td>
<td>E</td>
<td></td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Occurs on sandy soil often associated with sandstone, or on clay. Habitats are typically dry sclerophyll woodland communities, although heaths are also occupied.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Acacia gordonii</td>
<td></td>
<td>E</td>
<td>E</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Restricted to the north-west of Sydney, it has a disjunct distribution occurring in the lower Blue Mountains in the west, and in the Maroota/Glenorie area in the east. Grows in dry sclerophyll forest and heathlands amongst or within rock platforms on sandstone outcrops.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely – outside of species known distribution</td>
</tr>

<td>Eucalyptus camfieldii</td>
<td>Camfield's Stringybark</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>
<td>Restricted distribution in a narrow band with the most northerly records in the Raymond Terrace area south to Waterfall. Poor coastal country in shallow sandy soils overlying Hawkesbury sandstone. Coastal heath mostly on exposed sandy ridges.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Eucalyptus fracta</td>
<td>Broken Back Ironbark</td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td>Confined largely to State Forest. Locally common but restricted to the northern Broken Back Range near Cessnock, NSW. The dominant tree in a narrow band along the upper edge of a sandstone escarpment. Occurs in dry eucalypt woodland in shallow soils.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>

<td>Dichanthium setosum</td>
<td>Bluegrass</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>BioNet</td>
<td></td>

Associated with heavy basaltic black soils and red-brown loams with clay subsoil.<br>This species has not been recorded within the NSW BioNet Database within 10km of the subject site in the past 10 years.</td>
<td>Unlikely – outside of species known distribution</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="8"><b>Listed Threatened Ecological Communities</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">Coastal Swamp Oak (Casuarrine glauca) Forest of New South Wales and South East Queensland ecological community</td>
<td></td>
<td>E</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>This community is found on the coastal floodplains of NSW. It has a dense to sparse tree layer in which Casuarina glauca (swamp oak) is the dominant species northwards from Bermagui.</td>
<td>Unlikely – habitat absent from site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">Coastal Upland Swamp in the Sydney Basin Bioregion</td>
<td>E</td>
<td>E</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Coastal Upland Swamps occur primarily on impermeable sandstone plateaux with shallow groundwater aquifers in the headwaters and impeded drainage lines of streams, and on standstone benches with abundant seepage moisture. The Coastal Upland Swamp is generally associated with soils that are acidic and vary from yellow to grey mineral sandy loams with a shallow organic horizon to highly organic spongy black peats with pallid subsoils.</td>
<td>Unlikely – commonly associated soil type does not occur on site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">Castlereagh Scribbly Gum Woodland in the Sydney Basin Bioregion</td>
<td>V</td>
<td>E</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Occurs almost exclusively on soils derived from Tertiary alluvium, or on sites located on adjoining shale or Holocene alluvium.</td>
<td>Unlikely – site outside of this communities known distribution</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">Cooks River/Castlereagh Ironbark Forest in the Sydney Basin Bioregion</td>
<td>E</td>
<td>CE</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Has a very restricted natural distribution and mainly occurs on clay soils derived from the deposits of ancient river systems (alluvium), or on shale soils of the Wianamatta Shales.</td>
<td>Unlikely – site outside of this communities known distribution</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">Eastern Suburbs Banksia Scrub in the Sydney Basin Bioregion</td>
<td>CE</td>
<td>E</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Occurs on disjunct patches of nutrient poor aeolian (wind blown) dune sand.</td>
<td>Unlikely – commonly associated soil type does not occur on site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">Posidonia australis seagrass meadows of the Manning-Hawkesbury ecoregion</td>
<td></td>
<td>E</td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td>The ecological community is the assemblage of plants, animals and micro-organisms associated<br>with seagrass meadows dominated by Posidonia australis occurring in the warm temperate<br>Manning Shelf and Hawkesbury Shelf bioregions<br>on the east coast of Australia.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">Shale Sandstone Transition Forest in the Sydney Basin Bioregion</td>
<td>CE</td>
<td>CE</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Occurs at the edges of the Cumberland Plain, where clay soils from the shale rock intergrade with earthy and sandy soils from sandstone, or where shale caps overlay sandstone.</td>
<td>Unlikely – commonly associated geology does not occur on site.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">Subtropical and Temperate Coastal Saltmarsh</td>
<td></td>
<td>V</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>The physical environment for the ecological community is coastal areas under regular or<br>intermittent tidal influence. In southern latitudes saltmarsh is often the main vegetation-type in<br>the intertidal zone and commonly occurs in association with estuaries.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">Turpentine-Ironbark Forest of the Sydney Basin Bioregion</td>
<td>E</td>
<td>CE</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>Open forest, with dominant canopy trees including Turpentine Syncarpia glomulifera, Grey Gum Eucalyptus punctata, Grey Ironbark E. paniculata and Thin-leaved Stringybark E. eugenoides. Occurs close to the shale/sandstone boundary on the more fertile shale influenced soils, in higher rainfall areas on the higher altitude margins of the Cumberland Plain, and on the shale ridge caps of sandstone plateaus.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">Western Sydney Dry Rainforest and Moist Woodland on Shale</td>
<td></td>
<td>CE</td>
<td></td>
<td>PMST</td>
<td>The ecological community has a highly restricted distribution in the Sydney Basin bioregion<br>in New South Wales. It is confined to sheltered slopes and gullies on steeply sloping, rugged<br>topography mostly in the Cumberland Plain sub-region. The ecological community is generally limited to elevations below 300 metres above sea level<br>(asl) in gullies, sheltered slopes and rugged terrain. The soils on which the ecological<br>community occurs are almost exclusively clay soils derived from Wiannamatta Group shales.</td>
<td>Unlikely –suitable habitat absent from site</td>
</tr>

Appendix L Heritage Handbook

### L1 Heritage Handbook

### L1.1 Objectives of this Handbook

This Heritage Handbook is Appendix L to the Snapper Island (the Site) HMP and has been prepared as a standalone handbook that can be extracted from the main document for those responsible for the continuing care of this historically important place. It presents heritage principles to align with the best practice Australian heritage guidelines, the Burra Charter (Australia ICOMOS 2013), maintenance protocols, recommended inspection frequencies and some basic inspection techniques. This is followed by a tabulated report on ERM’s maintenance inspection findings and a program of recommended works.

Conservation Dos and Don’ts applicable to the care of this significant site are also presented to provide guidance for tradespeople, maintenance supervisors and building tenants. Adopting and applying these guidelines should ensure the historic place remains well cared for and in its current good condition with little or no impact on its heritage values. The 2013 Burra Charter is accessible at http://australia.icomos.org/publications/charters/ for ease of reference.

### L1.2 Seek Specialist Advice As Needed

Where proposed works could have an adverse impact on the heritage values of the Site, and guidance is not available in this Handbook or Appendix A of the HMP, seek advice from a suitably qualified and experienced heritage specialist.

#### L1.2.1 Heritage Values

#### L1.2.2 Heritage Status

The Site is currently included on the CHL for historical, associative, aesthetic and social heritage values. The citation for this listing is provided at Appendix B of the HMP for ease of reference.

#### L1.2.3 Statement of Significance

The Snapper Island Statement of Significance is provided below for context.

Snapper Island, is Commonwealth heritage listed for its historical, associative, aesthetic and social heritage values. The site comprises the original sandstone area, fore and aft areas of made ground, a range of utilitarian buildings and maritime structures, is historically important as the primary expression of the Navy League UK (NSW Branch). The Navy League UK (NSW Branch) was established at Drummoyne in 1921 by Len Forsythe, who saw the need to establish a voluntary training scheme for young boys, as naval cadets. The Sea Cadet movement is believed to be one of the oldest youth movements in the English-speaking world. The Snapper Island facility was officially opened on 26 November 1932 by Sir Charles Cox, on behalf of the Minister for Defence, as a living memorial to HMAS Sydney. HMAS Sydney, the first Royal Australian Navy ship to sink an enemy warship, the Emden, had been an inspiration to Forsythe. Snapper Island was the headquarters of Sydney's naval cadet groups and the Navy League from 1932 to 1977, and as such provided a voluntary step towards recruitment in the Australian Navy. Snapper Island has remained unoccupied since 2007.

Today, Snapper Island represents a unique landscape element within the former naval core of Sydney Harbour. The utilitarian buildings, constructed by the Navy League Cadets, retain their original layout and provide a tangible link to the training ethos implemented by Forsythe. The form of the island, with its land modifications, is a rare and interesting element, which inspires public interest and increases its aesthetic values.

Snapper Island does not have potential National or World heritage values.

### L1.3 Dos and Don’ts

The following Dos and Don’ts are intended to guide activities affecting the Site, and outline advice regarding appropriate and inappropriate maintenance materials and techniques, which may be considered for future works.

#### L1.3.1 Dos and Don’ts – General

<table>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do:</b></td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t:</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> check the conservation management documents and consult the HMP before proceeding with any maintenance works..</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> proceed with any maintenance works without being fully informed of the conservation issues and potential risks of not following the recommended processes.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> The heritage management documents are an important tool in the management of historic properties.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> apply Burra Charter principles. In particular:<br>• Do understand significance when making decisions about changing the historic fabric.<br>• Take a cautious approach and change as much as is necessary and as little as possible.<br>• Do carry out all work in a logical order.<br>• Do prepare records of the work undertaken.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> proceed with work without an understanding of the principles and practices established by the Burra Charter, which is the recognised heritage industry best practice in Australia.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> The conservation of historic building must be based on a thorough understanding of heritage values and the correct conservation approach.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> undertake a thorough investigation of proposed changes to heritage fabric to identify the potential for any impact on heritage values. If any doubt remains, seek the advice of a heritage specialist.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> undertake works where there is an unknown risk to heritage fabric that may lead to irreversible damage.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> It is important not to make changes that irreversibly damage historic properties.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> ensure that significant views to important historic buildings are retained.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> construct new buildings in locations that may impact on significant views.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> The aesthetic values of the Site can potentially be impacted by poorly designed and positioned new buildings.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> understand the physical fabric of the place as this in itself tells the story of the place’s history and historical significance.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> attempt to repair or conceal every knock or dent in the historic fabric as evidence of the use of a historic structure can be an important part of its history and contributes to its “patina” or quality of age.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> The fabric of a historic building tells its story and embodies the story of its occupants over time.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> ensure historic buildings have a compatible use.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> introduce uses into historic buildings that require changes to original layouts or that may put their security at risk..</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Unoccupied buildings attract unauthorised access by vandals and remove them from annual recurrent maintenance cycles leading to the loss of their heritage values.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> only repair as much of the historic fabric as is necessary rather than total replacement.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> replace existing elements with modern profiles as the significance of historic structures is linked to their traditional detailing.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> The more that is removed and replaced on a historic building, the more its integrity and consequently its heritage value is reduced.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> carefully piece in new work respecting the original fabric. Original and early building elements tell us about past construction techniques and styles and are an irreplaceable resource.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> remove evidence of original or features such as paint colour schemes and brackets. Leave the evidence and work around it.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> The more that is removed and replaced on a historic building, the more its integrity and consequently its heritage value is reduced.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> ensure termite or pest inspections are regularly undertaken and any activity treated by a licensed pest inspector.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> miss regular termite inspections.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Termites can wreak havoc in a heritage building in a short time.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> replace damaged glazing to match the original thickness and type of drawn glass where it is safe to do so.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> use plate, float, tinted or laminated glass in doors and windows unless a safety issue is apparent.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Safety glass is double the thickness of original glass and requires rebate depths to be increased.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> retain original and early fittings and fixtures if these survive.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> remove or replace original door and window furniture, light switches, power switches or light fittings where these are present.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> The significance of historic buildings is linked to their original fittings.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> repair historic materials with the same or similar materials – “like with like”. If the same material is no longer available or unsuitable, seek the most compatible option.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> use dissimilar materials for repair of historic buildings.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> The introduction of a modern material into historic fabric may cause long term damage.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> retain original planning and construction and heritage values embodied in a place’s historic fabric.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> remove evidence of original planning, construction systems, door and window furniture, or services (e.g. Closed off doorways, redundant door furniture or light fittings). Leave the evidence and work around it.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Evidence of past building layout and technologies can tell us how a place was used.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> maintain all fixings and hardware in clean and operative condition.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> bypass operating heritage features such as double hung window mechanisms.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Continued use will ensure regular care whereas redundancy may lead to removal and potential impact on heritage values.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> retain historic building elements on site during any conservation processes.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> remove historic building elements from site unless absolutely necessary. If removal is required ensure there is a process in place to ensure the physical care and security of the remained element is maintained.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Historic building elements can be damaged in transit, or lost or stolen during repair works.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> keep historic buildings clean internally and externally.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> avoid cleaning historic buildings as this can accelerate decay.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Well cared for fabric will decay more gradually.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> replace missing elements to historic buildings.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> allow elements to deteriorate and break.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> The retention of integrity supports the protection of heritage values.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> keep unpainted materials such as brick and stone unpainted.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> paint elements that have not been previously painted.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Painting can change the performance of the material and also can impact on a place’s aesthetic values.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> repaint painted elements with appropriate blends. This may involve additional investigation or consultation to determine the best alternative.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> paint elements with modern acrylic paints unless this is an appropriate alternative to the material to be painted.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Painting with inappropriate blends can change the performance of the material and lead to accelerated deterioration.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> have an understanding of significant fabric of an element prior to undertaking any work.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> damage or remove significant fabric.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> The physical fabric of the element is important as it tells the story of the architect’s intent.</td>
</tr>
</table>

#### L1.3.2 Dos and Don’ts – Roofs and Rainwater Goods

<table>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do:</b></td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t:</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> inspect the condition of the roofs every year for minor problems such as lifting of flashings, loose fixings, and damp roof timbers internally.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> ignore minor problems, as they can lead to larger and more costly problems if left unattended.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> The care of the roof on any building is vitally important especially for a historic building as unattended leaking can put rare and fragile interiors at risk.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> ensure that roofs, gutters and downpipes are regularly painted using a paint system that is consistent, if stable, with the earlier coatings.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> allow failed paintwork to expose metal elements.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Painting protects metal roofing and materials and extends their life.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> ensure vegetation growing behind the rainwater pipes or underneath or behind the gutters is cut back or removed altogether.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t:</b> allow vegetation growth to impact on the fabric of historic buildings.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Vegetation can help trap moisture against the rainwater goods and speed up their deterioration. Growing creepers can dislodge or damage gutters and pipes.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> be proactive and check externally in wet weather for places where the wall of the building is getting saturated. This is a sign that the rainwater system is leaking or blocked. In dry weather, there will be stains or marks left where water has been seeping into the wall.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> ignore potential leaking.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Rain leaking into the wall speeds up the deterioration any linings, can cause rot in internal timberwork, and can damage internal decoration and fittings.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> replace existing rainwater goods with galvanised steel to match the existing profile.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> introduce new materials or profiles.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Incompatible metals can cause a reaction with other metals in the system, for example zincalume with galvanised steel.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> regularly clear gutters and downpipes.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> allow leaf litter or bird droppings to accumulate on roofs of the building.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Blockage of the rainwater disposal system can cause internal flooding and damage historic fabric.</td>
</tr>
</table>

#### L1.3.3 Dos and Don’ts – Maintenance and Repair of Structural Timber

<table>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do:</b></td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t:</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> retain original timber members and detailing. Consider splicing in new sections of matching or similar species or strengthen original sections with “sister” members or metal plating.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> discard original timber members without considering retention options.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> The more that is removed and replaced on a historic building, the more its integrity and consequently its heritage value is reduced.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> install replacement timber elements if required with a matching profile and species. Where species is no longer available select a timber with similar characteristics.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> discard original timber members without considering retention options.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> The more that is removed and replaced on a historic building, the more its integrity and consequently its heritage value is reduced.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> retain original construction detailing when making repairs.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> introduce new detailing that is inconsistent with the original detail.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> The more that is removed, replaced or changed on a historic building, the more its integrity and consequently its heritage value is reduced.</td>
</tr>
</table>

#### L1.3.4 Dos and Don’ts – Maintenance and Repair of Corrugated Galvanised Iron and Metal Work

<table>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do:</b></td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t:</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> keep roofing, rainwater goods and exposed CGI clean</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> allow dirt, grime or debris to remain on roofs or against walls.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Dirt and other debris can accelerate the depletion of the zinc coating on CGI panels, leading to loss of protection and deterioration of fabric.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> consider patching smaller holes in CGI panels using appropriate materials, such as lead soldering or silicone resin.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> patch with incompatible materials, or attempt to patch larger holes which will show obvious signs of disturbance.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Patches can extend the life of a panel and may reduce the need to replace; however, use of appropriate materials is essential to ensuring longevity.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> consider the profile and visual impact of replacement panels when undertaking repair/replacement works.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> replace panels with alternate materials which are not in keeping with the aesthetic value, or with incompatible materials.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Replacement panels need to have similar materials performance and be in keeping with the aesthetic values of the place.</td>
</tr>
</table>

#### L1.3.5 Dos and Don’ts – Maintenance and Repair Brick and Stonework (Building Piers, Stairs and Seawalls)

<table>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do:</b></td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t:</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> regularly inspect stonework and brick pillars for signs of damage, salt attack and instability.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> allow stonework and brick to be continually attacked by salt, rising damp or other deteriorating contaminants.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Brick and stonework required specialist conservation skills to repair. As such, it is easier and more cost effective to undertake preventative maintenance.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> obtain similar and compatible materials to undertake repairs of stonework and brick elements.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> replace stonework or brick elements with modern materials which do not have similar material performance</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Material performance is important in maintaining a place in good condition. This can be compromised by use of incompatible materials.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> install appropriate drainage against buildings.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> have hard concrete surfaces abutting buildings.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> Concrete against masonry or stone building elements can increase the potential for rising damp.</td>
</tr>
</table>

#### L1.3.6 Dos and Don’ts – Landscape

<table>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do:</b></td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t:</b></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> carry out annual arborist inspections of the Cabbage Trees and undertake trimming where recommended for the health of the Tree.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> undertake any work on the Cabbage Trees without guidance from an arborist.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> The Cabbage Trees requires specialist care to support their healthy growth to reach its maximum life span.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> continue regular grounds maintenance of the gardens bed and grassed areas.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> allow vegetation to become overgrown and compromise the built structures.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> The gardens and grassed areas can become overgrown and lead to vermin infestation and damage to built structures. This can be avoided through regular gardening and landscaping work.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><b>Do</b> undertake regular inspections of the sea walls to assess damage and need for repairs.</td>
<td></td>
<td><b>Don’t</b> allow the sea walls to deteriorate beyond repair.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4"><b>WHY?</b> The seawalls are an important part of the history of the island, relating directly to the development of the unique shape and layout. The seawalls need to be maintained to protect these values.</td>
</tr>
</table>

### L1.4 Unforseen Discoveries

It is relatively unlikely that the sub-surface areas of site contain items of archaeological interest that may be discovered during works on the Island given the extent of sub-surface disturbance involved in the construction of the Site. However, the buildings themselves provide some potential for unforeseen discovery of archaeological items.

The cultural heritage analysis of the Site (see Appendix I) has determined that it has low Aboriginal archaeological potential. However, if potential Aboriginal or historic archaeological items are found during site works, the following procedure should be implemented:

Cease work in the immediate area;

Notify the Site Manager and the owner;

Photograph the item in situ;

Inspect the item with the owner’s Heritage Advisor and determine if consultation is required and if the item can be safely moved for further examination and works can continue;

Prepare a brief report documenting the item’s location, including in situ photographs, and recommendations for management and future storage;

If the item cannot be moved without further archaeological excavation and recording, engage a suitably qualified archaeologist to record and assess the item. Determine if adjacent works need to be amended to protect the item in consultation with the owner and relevant Commonwealth and State officers as necessary; and

Implement the guidance of the owner’s Heritage Advisor and Commonwealth and State, including ongoing requirements for the item’s protection as required.

In the event of the discovery of human remains, the Police will also need to be contacted.

A full Chance Finds Protocol for both Aboriginal and Historic Heritage is provided at Appendix J.

### L1.5 Maintenance of Heritage Significance

### L1.6 Maintenance Protocols

This section provides maintenance protocols to guide regular inspection, Catch-Up Maintenance Schedule, Cyclical Preventative Maintenance Inspection Schedule and Planned Maintenance Works that ensures heritage values are conserved.

The Site has not been subject to regular, effective maintenance, which has contributed to its good condition overall.

#### L1.6.1 Reasons for Maintenance

The desirable standard of maintenance depends on the intensity of use and climatic conditions. A policy of preventative maintenance work is less expensive in every way. Neglecting maintenance leads to decay and may lead to costly repair or the building replacement cycle being the only viable option.

A clear understanding of the building materials, construction and its development, and as a result the likely problems that may be encountered, assists with the anticipation and appropriate maintenance and repair. Maintenance is defined by the Burra Charter as the continuous protective care of the fabric, contents and setting of a place. Preventative Maintenance is carried out to prevent an item failing or wearing out by providing systematic inspection, detection and prevention of damage or failure, and is usually programmed. If carried out properly, preventative maintenance will reduce the probability of decay. This contrasts with corrective maintenance, whereby damaged or deteriorating material is replaced or repaired. Preventative maintenance can not only reduce, or even avoid, the need for repairs later; it will prevent the loss of original fabric and is cost-effective. Corrective maintenance is disruptive and costly in both significant fabric and financial terms (SPAB: Technical Guide 10).

Regular inspections and preventative maintenance thus form the basis of the most cost effective approach to the conservation of buildings and items of heritage significance. While some heritage buildings and items have materials that may require specialised care, the majority of preventative maintenance is standard housekeeping that can be undertaken as part of programmed maintenance activities for all assets.

Regular inspection and preventative maintenance activities can contribute to extending the life of an asset and reduce the whole of life costs associated with using the building. The need for major conservation work is most commonly the result of insufficient inspection and maintenance. The cost benefits of developing and implementing an inspection and preventative maintenance regime is outlined in graph below (Figure L-1).

Continuation of existing uses and seeking new or adaptive re-use of heritage items also plays a key role in conserving heritage values by ensuring maintenance issues are identified early and supporting justification for funding works for ongoing care.

Figure L‑1 Preventative Maintenance Costs Diagram (NSW Heritage Office 2004:3)

[image: A two axis graph showing repair costs arrow on the vertical and time in years arrow on the horizontal.]

#### L1.6.2 Regular Inspection

Regular annual inspections of Snapper Island should be undertaken by the landowner/manager. These inspections are important as part of the ongoing care of the heritage values and fabric of the buildings and the associated elements on the island. Additional guidance for these inspections is provided below.

Maintenance and examination of the Site should ideally be tackled by routines of six monthly, annual and five yearly inspections, followed by brief reports. The inspections should be undertaken by the Facilities Manager and a heritage practitioner with knowledge of historic buildings and conservation of building materials and methods. These should be integrated into annual inspections.

Examination of the fabric of the building and its surrounds should be conducted systematically by circulating around the site and building externally and internally in a clockwise direction. Examiners should use their senses to observe and note building and surrounding environment condition and defects.

The checklist provided below is an indication of what needs to be considered for Snapper Island to ensure issues that may have an impact on heritage values are mitigated. This checklist should also be integrated into the owner’s inspection checklist.

#### L1.6.3 Maintenance Plans

This section presents prioritised implementation plans comprising specific works tasks to manage the heritage values of the Site and individually significant features. These works should be integrated into the Finance and PSP maintenance plans, minor capital expenditure programs or capital expenditure programs.

The maintenance plans are prioritised according to the risk framework and divided into:

• Catch-Up Maintenance;

• Cyclical Preventative Maintenance Schedule; and

• Planned Works.

##### L1.6.3.1 Catch Up Maintenance

A schedule of catch-up maintenance and repairs is provided in the Heritage Asset Management Schedules (Appendix A) and in Table 7‑1 of the main HMP, including general housekeeping and maintenance issues. Buildings that have been assessed as structurally unsound have ‘make safe’ actions listed in the event that these buildings are retained into the short and medium term.

The repairs are prioritised under three categories:

• High (complete within 6 months) – work considered essential for the safety of the site or the users and visitors;

• Medium (complete within 12 months) – recommended work of lower priority but necessary to minimise long- term damage to the site’s fabric and to enable the continued use of the site; and

• Low (Complete within 2 years) - work necessary to maintain the condition of the place and its heritage values.

##### L1.6.3.2 Cyclical Preventative Maintenance Schedule

The following checklist is an indication of what needs to be considered for the Site to ensure issues that may have an impact on heritage values are mitigated. The following should also be integrated into the owner’s inspection checklist.

Six-month cyclical maintenance plan

• check plant growth over island and prune/mow where necessary;

• remove vegetation build-up from around edges of buildings;

• inspect Cabbage Trees for damage or die-back;

• monitor animal/vermin activity and place additional traps/baits where necessary;

• check all bird barriers for damage and replace where necessary;

• inspect termite activity and treat where necessary;

• check external windows and doors for damage and repair as necessary;

• check for graffiti/other signs that the island has been illegally accessed;

• check that roofs are watertight, and patch and repair where necessary with compatible materials;

• check rainwater goods to ensure they are free of leaves and other debris; and

• conduct basic cleaning to ensure condition does not continue to deteriorate.

Annual cyclical maintenance plan

• remove rubbish or unwanted material and clean the interiors of each building;

• check opening and closing of all doors and windows and ease and lubricate as required;

• check the condition of stairs, seawalls and retaining walls;

• check condition of all directional and interpretive signage (where available);

• check condition and integrity of all contributory elements of significance identified in Table 5‑4;

• inspect roofs (outside and inside);

• check and clear gutters, downpipes, stormwater pits and drains;

• apply rust treatment to all external metal elements and repaint as required;

• repair any broken glazing, reapply putty to loose panes, and clean windows and painted surrounds;

• repair and repaint any damaged timber elements;

• replace any damaged interior cladding sheets;

• horticulturalist to inspect health and condition of Cabbage Tree;

• clean light fittings and change bulbs or fluorescent tubes; and

• check fire extinguishers and replace as required.

5 yearly cyclical maintenance plan

Every five years the heritage practitioner should make a full report especially noting any structural defects that should be kept under observation and the cyclical maintenance plan should be updated, drawing attention to any defects that should be kept under observation or where further study might be required. Proposed actions should be prioritised and divided into the following categories and recorded in a log book:

• small items (basically good housekeeping);

• should building upgrades occur, requirement for repairs to mechanical, electrical, plumbing and drainage services;

• a rolling program of long-term preventive maintenance carried out year by year; and

• major items of conservation such as roofing, walls, windows, doors and floor coverings and services.

The scope of a typical five yearly inspection would be consistent with the list of items set out in both the six monthly and the annual inspection.

#### L1.6.3.3 Reporting and Review Protocols

Planned and unscheduled maintenance works and alterations to heritage items and areas need to be recorded by the PSP and Finance to provide a centralised record of changes to the place over time, and to satisfy Finance’s heritage register requirements under the EPBC Act. Electronic recording should capture any maintenance undertaken at Snapper Island with attention to significant fabric (refer to Figure Figure 5‑1 in the HMP for significance of site elements). These records are to be incorporated in future updates of the statutory HMP.

In the event of divestment, a copy of site works records should be made available to the new site manager, and a record should continue to be maintained for the site.

For this HMP to remain a useful heritage management and conservation tool it needs to be reviewed and updated at regular intervals. The review is to be undertaken by Finance, with assistance from a heritage specialist as required.

The review is to focus on amending responsibilities, include commentary on any works undertaken since the last HMP review; it will also consider any changes or planned changes of use and the interpretation strategy.

Whilst Snapper Island remains a Commonwealth property, in accordance with the EPBC Act, this HMP must be reviewed at least once every five years. The review should assess the content of the plan and determine its effectiveness in protecting the heritage values of the place and make recommendations for updating or re-writing the plan as required.

Finance/PSP will check the implementation of the HMP, including actions identified in Table 7‑1 and cyclical maintenance schedule through Finance’s annual Property Asset Management Plan (PAMP) process, with corrective actions to be recorded and implemented within agreed timeframes.
